1271. MARY CULLEN was indicted for unlawfully, maliciously, and feloniously cutting and wounding Catharine Sullivan on her head, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
CATHARINE SULLIVAN . The prisoner is my mother. I was at her room, in Half Moon-street, Bishopsgate, on the 2nd of May, with my brother and sister—I was cutting a bit of bread for my sister, and my mother took a knife and dug it into my head three times—she took my shawl off my neck, and said, "Is there any blood on it?"—I said, "No, mother"—she took her bonnet and shawl, and went out.
Prisoner. Q. What did I hit you with? A. After you cut my head you hit me with your pocket—you cut my head about eight o'clock on Sunday night.
THOMAS BUCKLEY . I went in, and saw this child crying—there was a little blood about her.
THOMAS PORTER . I am a surgeon. I examined the prosecutrix—there were three incised wounds on the crown of her head—one of them was deep—she was ill about a fortnight—she is hardly well now.
Prisoner's Defence. On the 1st of May, I awoke my child, and wanted her to go to Mile End-road on an errand; I turned to the baby, and in the meantime she took my pocket from under my head; it had two pence, and two sixpences in silver, in it; I took the pocket out of her hand, and hit her with it, being in a passion; she is constantly plundering, and if she sees a neighbour's door open she will go in and rob them; on the 2nd of May I went out to work, and left her 1 s. to get some coals; on my return home she gave me 8 d. change; I went up stairs, and the person on the first floor said, "Is not your girl a nice article, to rob me of my coals;" I came down; she was sitting on a stool by
the table; being in a passion, I hit her, and her head fell against the corner of the table; I did not know her was cut, or I should have looked after✗ it before I went out, for she sat down and had her tea with me; she is a shocking child, for though her my husband has left me with six children; I✗ heard my husband was in Birmingham; I went to if could find him, and I was given into custody.
GUILTY .*— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18470510-1272]
1272. JAMES M'LEAN and CHARLES GARDNER was indicated for unlawfully obtaining money by false pretences.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
ISAAC SAMUEL ROSENBAUM . I am a leather-seller and slipper-manufacturer, and live at No. 8, Old Gravel-lane. On Sunday morning, the 25th of April, nine Germans arrived from Germany, on their way to New York—they came to lodge at my house—there were a great many Germans them✗—I spoke to Mr. Ireland, and I saw the two prisoners in his parlour✗ and they made an agreement with nine German emigrants to take them on✗ to New York—M'Lean said he was the agent, and Gardner was the captain of the barque Recovery —Gardner said he was the captain, and he would auls✗ 5 l. a head from the emigrants to take them to New York—Gardner made the agreement, and M'Lean wrote the agreement, and told them the usual practice✗ was to play 1 l. down, when they got on board to pay the remaindy✗—I saw all those nine persons pay Garden 1 l. deposit—they were julirs✗ Mendel, Emanuel Moritz, Joseph Rothschild, Isaack Guelder, Fanny roths✗ child, Ferdinand Haas, Sarah Klemberg, Joseph Keller, and Sarah Moritsnot one of them could speak English—I explained to them, before they parted with the money, the situation of the prisoners, that Gardner was the captain of the ship Recovery, and that M'Lean was the agent—they told me so, and I told the emigrants so—I saw the money paid—it was some French✗ money, and some Dutch money—M'Lean and Gardner asked me the worth✗ of it, and they took it—I did not ask Gardner that night where he lived—I did another day—I asked him for his card—he said he had not got a printed card—he said he lived at No. 16, King-street, Tower-hill—I said I knew that Mr. Williams, a provision-merchant, lived there—Gardner said✗ it was a mistake—I went to Mr. Williams', and found Gardner did not live✗ there—I went back to Mr. Ireland's, and said, "I suppose this is only a swindle"—the prisoners afterwards came to Mr. Ireland's house—I went to them, and said, "Give these emigrants their money back, I think everything will be settled"—they refused—Gardner said, "I did not touch the money, M'Lean took the money"—I said, "You gave them a receipt, not M'Lean; they have to do with you now, not M'Lean"—M'Lean said, "Did I promise you!"—I said, "Gardner promised to take them to New York"—I am quite sure Gardner was represented as the captain, and M'Lean as the agent—they took these people on board, and showed them a ship, but I was not at home at the time.
M'Lean. Q. I thing you know a person named Ireland? A. Yes, he lives within a few doors of me in Old Gravel-lane—he sent for me on the Saturday evening—I found you in house—he said you were a shipping agent, he had known you a length of time, and you were capable of doing justice to the fullest extent—he told me you were an honest man—I called on you the next morning—I do not know what time it was—you went to black.
wall with me—I cannot tell how long you stopped there—you stopped till the second steam-boat arrived from Rotterdam, in which these emigrants came—I said there was no occasion for you to wait longer—I went with the Germans, and you came home—I do not recollect your being introduced to, Germans in my house—I sometimes have company—it is very likely you were there—perhaps I might have come to your house in Sidney-street on the Monday—you did not introduce me to Captain Gardner in your parlour—I did not see him before I saw him in Mr. Ireland's house—I was not introduced to Caption Gardner in your parlour to make arrangements at so much a-head for those emigrants—I did not arrange for them to be taken at 5 l. head, and for me to receive 6 s. a-head for introducing them—you said, "You shall have 6 s. a-head," and I did not answer it—I cannot tell whether you were going to do this for nothing—I did not employ you to get a ship for these emigrants—Mr. Ireland said you were a shipping agent, you could recommend a ship for those emigrants—I did not make arrangements to receive 6 s. a-head.
COURT. Q. Were you to have 6 s. a-head profit for your trouble? A. No, M'Lean said, "I will pay you 6 s."—I did not answer him.
M'Lean. Q. Did I say I would give you 6 s.? A. Yes, promised I should have 6 s.—I made no arrangements for the Germans to go down and look at the ship—the emigrants made an agreement with Gardner—I never saw Mr. Gardner in your house—I saw him at Mr. Ireland's, who sent word to me that a shipping agent he knew was in his house—I went there and saw you and Mr. Gardner—I gave Gardner in charge on the Wednesday afterwards—I went to the station to explain the transaction, and said you were partners together.
Gardner. Q. Did you not meet me at Mr. M'Lean's house? A. No, I never saw you there—I was not in M'Lean's house till I had seen you.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you get back the 9 l.? A. No.
GEORGE IRELAND . I keep the Old Duke William public-house. Mr. Rosenubaum told me there were some emigrants arrived, and he wanted to ship them off as there was such a number of them—I had known M'Lean two or three years off and on, acting as a shipping agent—I spoke to him—he said he would do what he could, and on the Thursday night I saw him and Gardner and the emigrants in my parlour—I did not hear anything pass between them—I afterwards went to the barque Recovery because Rosenbaum seemed to signify there was something wrong—I inquired for Captain Gardner—I found there was no such person—M'Lean had brought Gardner to my house—he had a glass or two of porter, and I said to him, "I hope when I come on board your ship I shall have some wine, better then porter"—I said that because he had represented himself as captain of the barque Recovery —he did so in the presence of M'Lean, who had brought him there—I had a box in my charge, but it is in charge of the police now—M'Lean gave it to my son while I was gone on board the Recovery —M'Lean said to my son, in my presence, "There is money in the box, I must have it back."
COURT. Q. Is there a vessel called the Recovery? A. Yes, I went on board, and inquired if Captain Charles Gardner was on board—they said they did not know such a man—the vessel is not fitting up for emigrants—Captain Gibson is the captain—she is not going to New York—she is going to the North for coals.
M'Lean. Q. You have known me for there years? A. Yes—we had no transaction only as a customer—I said if you would give your attention to
get a ship for these people it would pay you well—you did not see your way✗ clear, and I spoke to other.
M'Lean. Q. After having done that, did you not come to me on a Saturday evening, and see me in my own parlour, and tell me there were from 60 to 80 passengers come, and 20,000 were coming over, and persuade me to do it, and you would introduce me to Rosenbaum, who was the agent? A. No, he is no agent, only an interpreter—he or anybody had the management—they wanted to get off quickly—you called on me, and I introduced you to Rosenbaum—it was only those persons who had not made their arrangement in Germany that you had to do with—you were in my house on Sunday evening—I do not recollect anybody being with you—I introduced you from your card—I have known you before my bar for two years, acting as a shipping agent—whether you have a license or not I cannot say—I introduced you to Rosenbaum—what arrangements took place I have nothing to do with—I only know I went to the barque Recovery, and found she was set✗ chartered for New York—it was at my instigation you undertook it, and if the thing had gone on properly, it would have been all right—I did it for the sake of the Germans, to get them off, because I knew they had no money is stop here.
JULIUS MENDELL ( through an interpreter.) I saw the two prisoners—gave a sovereign—I do not remember whether I laid it on the table—the two prisoners were present—I gave it for them, in consequence of what Mr. Roenbaun✗ told me—I expected to be taken to New York—I went down and saw✗ the ship—we walked through the Thames Tunnel to it—it was in some cand✗—I do not know the name of the dock—the two prisoners were with me—they showed me about the ship.
M'Lean. Q. Did you go with me on the Tuesday? A. I cannot tell the day—some others went with me—you introduced me to Captain Gardner who was with the carpenter, between decks, making the berths for passengers, as you said—I appointed to meet Captain Gardner, at three o'clock in the afternoon, to make arrangements.
ISAAC SAMUEL ROSENBAUM re-examined. I saw every one of the nine✗ persons pay their 1 l. each—M'Lean took some, and Gardner took some.
EMANUEL MORITZ . I paid 1 l. to go out with the Recovery —I paid a 10✗ guilder-piece and some shillings.
M'Lean. I acknowledge I saw the whole money paid, and put in the box.
NICHOLAS GIBSON . I live in Lower John-street, Commercial-road. I am✗ owner of the Recovery —she is lying in the Surrey-canal—you get to it by going through the Thames-tunnel—M'Lean called on me on the 26th of April—I had never seen him before—he made some offer about chartering the vessel—Gardner was not captain of the vessel, my brother was—I told him I would not allow the ship to go out without my brother being captain—if M'Lean stated that Gardner was captain of that vessel he stated what was wrong—I never heard of Gardner going on board my vessel and showing it, till I was at the police-court—there was nothing at all going on to prepares✗ the vessel for emigrants.
M'Lean. Q. From what passed between us, would you not suppose that I should be able to negotiate with you? A. You offered me a very excellent price—I told you if I found the partied respectable I might charter the ship—I received a note from you—this is it—( read —"No. 2, Sidney-street—Sir, according to promise I take the liberty of addressing you on the subject
of chartering the barque Recovery;—when I saw you I offered 8 s. per ton per month; I have this morning seen your brother, and offered him 10 s. per ton; and considering the age of the vessel, there is no doubt you will consider the offer sufficient; the vessel to be chartered for four months certain, at 10 s. a ton per month; the chartered pay the port charges, provision, and seamen's wages. Another vessel being offered, your early answer will oblige. Yours, &c."
M'Lean. Q. Did I not say if your brother took charge of her, it would be no objection? A. Yes—you would do anything to get the ship—my brother told me you had seen him, and arranged at 10 guineas a month—I told you I would not accept it till I had inquired as to the respectability of the party—you said you were only an agent in the matter—I do not know that you had a right to tell Gardner that you were likely to come to an agreement.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Ten shillings a ton would have been a famous freightage? A. Yes—I could not let my vessel for one-third of that.
M'Lean. Q. Did you not say you were getting 90 l. a month in the coal trade? A. Yes—I said I would consider it, and give you an answer in writing—you called on Thursday morning and told me Captain Gardner was in custody.
DAVID JOHNSON . I know the prisoner M'Lean—he is a journeyman baker and was in my employ up to Nov. last.
M'Lean. It was only for the time I was in your employ that you knew me in that capacity. Witness. No—may acquaintance with you took place last July—you represented that you knew me twenty years ago, but, to the best of my knowledge, I never saw you before.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Was he a good hand at journeyman baking? A. Yes, a very good one, the best in England, I think.
M'Lean Defence. I have endeavoured, as far as lay in my power, to carry out the views and do justice to every person that employed me; I admit these people paid their money; I saw it paid; it was done with the best intention; I made an arrangement with Gardner, to take sixty passengers out—about the 6th of May, I had met Captain Gardner, named to him the subject; he told me of the Recovery; he took me down to look at it, and wished me to wait on the owner; I waited on Mr. Gibson, and you have heard what he has said—with respect to Rosenbaum I was employed by him to do the best I possibly could to dispatch these emigrants, and I was to receive one-half of the profits—with respect to anything else, I leave it entirely in your own hands; I have got my conscience clear, and here I stand innocent of anything wrong—there never was fraud intended by Captain Gardner or me.
Gardner's Defence. I called on M'Lean on Saturday evening, the 24th of April; he mentioned having a number of passengers, and I thought it would suit my purpose; I called on a corn merchant in the City, and asked if he would join a charter party to transport these emigrants; he said it was a deviation from his business, but if it would further my views he had no objecttion; I turned my attention to a vessel called the Recovery; I commissioned Mr. M'Lean to call on the owner, and from what he said I was of opinion she could be chartered; on the Monday I was at Mr. Ireland's, Rosenbaum came in, and I named having a vessel about to proceed to New York; Rosenbaum asked, "What will you take these passengers for?" I named 5 l.; he said, "What commission will you allow me?" I think I said 4 s.; he said he was in the habit of having 6 s. from Phillips and Tiplady, if I would give him the same he would give me the preference; I agreed to it, and he seemed anxious✗
that they should see the vessel; I, knowing they wanted to get away, called on a friend, a shipwright, and asked him to walk on board and give me as estimate of what it would come to, to fit it up, in case the charter-party came to a close; they left me about eleven o'clock on Tuesday morning, and agreed to meet me at Mr. Ireland's at three o'clock; I met them, and instead of paying the 5 l. it was agreed that 1 l. should be paid then, and the remainder when they were on board; it made no difference to me, knowing I had a responsible party to join me in the charter; they paid 9 s.; the money was put into the box, and after stopping a short time, Mr. Rosenbaum and his daughter asked me to leave an agreement with them, as they might get more passengers; I did so, and the next morning I made an arrangement to proceed to Mr. Ireland's; I went to his house about one o'clock on the Wednesday; I left a message that I was on business in the corn-market, and should be back at three o'clock; I returned, and saw Mr. M'lean; I left him full power in negotiate anything that might occur; I called at Mr. Ireland's again in the evening; I was taken into custody; I have not had opportunity to get a solicitor; under these circumstances I applied to traverse my trail, and had his Lordship granted my request I should have had not only a solicitor but a Counsel; I have been a master and owner of a ship the last three years; I commanded a ship for Mr. Soames in 1844.
M'LEAN — GUILTY .
GARDNER — GUILTY .
Transported for Seven Years

1273. CHARLES MADELL was indicted for stealing 1 dressing-case value 15 s.; 1 pair of scissors, 1 s.; 2 razors, 3 s.; 2 brushes, 4 s.; 1 razors strop, 1 s.; 1 comb, 6 d.; 1 looking-glass, 2 s.; 1 knife, 6 d.; 2 bone rollers 4 s.; 1 pair of tweezers, 6 d.; 1 nail-file, 1 s.; and 1 bottle, 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of Samuel Ellett: and HENRY EDWARDS , for that he feloniously did incite, move, procure, counsel, hire, and command the said Charles Madell the same felony to do an commit.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL ELLETT. I am a dressing-case maker, and live in Queen-street, City-road. On the 17th of April the prisoner Madell came to my house—he said he was a dressing-case maker, that he had a customer from Chelmsford, and he wanted some good dressing-cases—he said he wanted better goods than he had got—I sent my son with him with three cases—my son and Madell came back—he ordered five cases, three of one sort and two of the other, and asked if I had got some envelope-boxes—I said, "Yes"—he selected some, and four dressing-cases, and my son went him to show them—he returned with an order for once of the dressing-cases, which was to be sent to a public-house in Old-street—I told my son not to leave anything without the money, on way account—my son brought back the bag with the samples, and when I looked into it, there was once dressing-case short—I got the rest of the order ready, and at nine o'clock in the evening I went with my son to Baltion✗ street—I found Madell had left, and Edwards was about to leave—my son went into the house—he came out again, and said I was to go home directly, for Madell had gone to my house—we hurried home, and he had not been then—I said, "We must go and look after these men"—we went, and in going along, we saw the prisoner Edwards—my son said, "That is the gentleman that represent himself as Mr. Whiffin, from Chelmsford"—I stopped him,
and said, "Are you Mr. Whiffin, from Chelmsford?"—he said, "No. I am a Londoner bred and born"—I said, "Where there?"—he then said, "I came from Wales"—I said, "You have been looking at some dressing-cases"—he said, "I have"—I took him to my house, and he pointed out the very dressing-cases that they had been looking at—this is the card that Madell left me, and he wrote on the back of it the name of Whiffin.
Madell. Q. I represented myself as a fancy cabinet-maker; I believe you know that is true? A. No.—you were a perfect stranger to me—you said you had done business for respectable people—you named a person in St. Paul's—churchyard—I inquired there, and they do not know you—you mentioned the name of Whiffin—I asked you what trade he was, and what you dealt in—the second time you came you showed me a printed paper—you said Mr. Whiffin paid ready money—I considered I was dealing with Mr. Whiffin.
FREDERICK CLEMENT ELLETT . I am the son of Samuel Ellett. On Saturday evening, the 17th of April, I saw Madell at my father's house—I took some dressing-cases to Madell's house—he went there and said, "My customer, Mr. Whiffin, is at the public-house over the way—we will and see him"—we went to the Sir John Falstaff, in Old-street, and there I saw Edwards, and Madell there gave me a glass of ale—I do not think they had spoken together before he gave it, but they were standing close to each other at the table—the ale made me feel a sort of dizziness in my head—I never drank any other ale that had the same effect on me—when I had had the ale, they began to look at the dressing-cases, and I heard Edwards say, "Will you take 3 l.?"—Madell said, "No"—they offered me some more ale, and they went our together—Madell returned by himself in two or these minutes, and said he would have five dressing-cases—he gave me this paper, and he said he would go back to my father, to know how soon they could be got ready—he went with me—he talked about Chelmsford—he said Mr. Whiffin was brother to Miss Whiffin, and that she was the principal person in the business—he said what a nice place Chelmsford was, and that Mr. Whiffin had his own horse and cart, and he wanted to drive down that night—I understood that he meant Mr. Edwards, the person I had seen—I had not heard him address Edwards by any name—when we got to my father's, the goods were looked out, and an order given to take some dressing-cases to Baltic-street—I took four dressing-cases and two envelope-boxes—when I got to Madell's house, he said, "My customer is here now, I will take them up stairs, and show them to him"—he took the bag up stairs, and brought it down in a few minutes—he gave me the bag, and said, "Bring the other dressing-cases at nine o'clock, to the public-house in Old-street"—I went home, and found one case was missing—I went at nine o'clock—Madell was gone—I saw Edwards standing at the bar—he said Madell was gone to our house—we went there, but could not find him.
Madell. Q. What time in the afternoon was it that I first had the dressing-cases? A. I cannot say—I should think between four and five o'clock—you did not say that Mr. Edward's name was Whiffin, but you talked of Mr. Whiffin in going along—you gave me a glass of ale—you drank out of another glass, not the same that I had—I went down stairs when I went into your house—you did not say you had got a case, and you would pay my father for that one with the others—I thought that one was in the bag, or I should not have left it—my father ordered me not—I brought the others, which were ordered, a little after nine—it was not more than a
quarter after nine—you did not tell me to bring a bill and receipt with them—I asked you if I should bring one—you said, "No"—you did not say, "Bring a bill for them and the one I have got"—you did not say a word about keeping one—I went back, and missed one—it is about ten minutes' walk from my father's house to yours—you did not say you was going to take the dressing-case out to show.
DAVID ABRAHAMS . I was at that time a City police-constable, No. 150. I took Edwards into custody at No. 8, Jewin-street—in going to the station, he said his name was Mr. Whiffin, and that he was an agent, at Chelmsford—I asked him if he knew a person named Madell—he said he had been in company with a friend who had made a dupe of him—when he got to the station he said his name was Edwards—I said, "You told me your name was Whiffin"—he said he had not.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How comes it you are not in the police now? A. I resigned—I was ordered to resign—I had been in the police before—Edwards and I were alone when he told me his name was Mr. Whiffin.
JOHN HARVEY . ( police-constable G 118.) I took Madell into custody—he said he did not steal the case, he had it in the regular way of business—he did not say whom he wanted to show it to—he said if I went to the George and Blue Boar in Holborn I should find a person of the name of Cook, who would give me the case—I went there, and could not find Cook—I found on✗ Madell, at the station, a letter, purporting to come from Charles Cook—this is it—( read —"To Mr. Madell,—Sir, I could not get in my rents from Colchester, or I would have sent you the account—I will be sure to sead✗ 17 l. some day next week; you can make me twelve desks,— CHARLES Cook.")—Madell also showed me a printed bill of Miss Whiffin's, and said this would satisfy me—I also found on him some bills drawn by Charles Madell, and accepted by Henry Edwards.
Madell. Q. On Sunday morning you took me, and searched my place? A. Yes—I found the dressing-case at your own house.
CHARLES GOODMAN . I know the prisoner Madell—there was a time when he was a partner of mine—it commenced last July, and ended in Dec. last—I know his handwriting—this letter, purporting to come from Mr. Cook, is Madell's writing—I am a commission-agent—when I was in partnership with Madell I was a fancy cabinet-maker.
MARY ANN WHIFFIN . I reside at Chelmsford with my sister. I do not know Madell—he had nothing to do with my business—this is one of my shop bills.
Madell. I called on you at Chelmsford. Witness. I never saw you before.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you a brother at Chelmsford? A. Yes, a house-agent and appraiser.
SAMUEL ELLETT re-examined. This is my dressing-case.
Madell's Defence. The dressing-case I am charged with stealing, is the one that the prosecutor wished me to show; I never named the name of Whiffin as a customer, but merely showed the circular to describe the sort of goods I sold—while I was I the prosecutor's shop, I stated that I had been through Chelmsford, Colchester, and Essex; I gave my right address; he knew nothing about Whiffin; on the day stated, Edwards and I dined at the Sir John Falstaff; I expected to meet the party the cases were for; he was in my debt, and I was to draw a bill on him; the landlord got me the stamp✗ and I did; I went to the Sir John Falstaff about nine o'clock; the witness
did not come; I left word with Edwards that I was gone out about money, and would call on him that night or early on Monday morning; the party I called on for money had nothing but a check for 20 l.; I had left this case behinds me on the road; this was all the same evening; I was at home by half-past ten, and next morning two policemen came and said I was in custody for a dressing-case; I said it was a fair sale, I had purchased the goods, and I should pay on Monday morning; I told the prosecutor if he would come with me I would take him to the person who would pay the money; I had not the slightest intention to cheat him—up to the last four months I had fourteen men at work on my premises; I had an execution in my premises, and 160 l. of stock was sold to pay 60 l.—the reason I did not give the prosecutor the name of my customer was, that I expected to see that person at the Sir John Falstaff; had the prosecutor waited till Monday, all would have been settled; Edwards never heard the name of Whiffin.
MADELL — GUILTY . Aged 33.— Transported for Seven Years.
[ see the next case. ]
EDWARDS — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470510-1274]
1274. CHARLES MADELL . and HENRY EDWARDS were again indicted for conspiracy.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
LEWIS COLLINS . I in Green's-terrace, Clerkenwell—I am a goldsmith—I manage the business of my father, Francis Collins—I have known Edwards many years as a watch-gilder and colourer. On the 10th of April he came to my shop, between six and seven o'clock in the evening—he asked if I had any gentlemen's short guards to show to a gentleman that he thought he could sell them to—I showed him some—he selected two, and said he would take them to show the gentleman—he came again, and said the gentleman had kept one, and he returned the other—he came again, and said the gentleman would prefer the one he returned to the one he kept—I let him have the other—he came again, and said the gentleman would keep the two at the price I had named—they were worth 4 l. 15 s.—I have seen them since at the pawnbroker's—when I saw Edwards at the station I asked him how he came to serve me so—he said he had not been able to see the gentleman.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You have known Edwards many years? A. Yes—I do not know that he was once in a very large way of business—I have known him ever since I can remember, doing little odd jobs—he was engaged in the watch gilding trade—when he came to me on that Saturday evening, he asked if I had any gentleman's short guards—he selected two of different patterns—he said he would show them to the gentleman and if the gentleman chose one he would bring me the other back, and bring the money for the one he kept.
JAMES SWAINE . I am shopman to Mr. Reeves, a pawnbroker, in Redcross-street—Madell pawned these two chains with me, in the name of John Cook, one on the 12th of April, for 1 l. 5 s., and the other on the 14th of April for 1 l. 1 s.—he came again on the 16th of April, and I advanced him 4 s. more.
RICHARD JOHN COVENTRY . I am a gold curb chain-maker, in Parceval-street Clerkenwell. About the 6th of March Edwards came to my shop—I had known him before—he brought Madell with him, and said he was a highly respectable tradesman in the habit of selling watches and chains, and he required some chains to show to a gentleman he was about selling a watch to—he said I might trust him—they selected three chains—Madell said he wanted them to show to a gentleman he was about selling a watch to—I said,
if one was sold I should expert cash—Madell said, "Certainly," and if not sold they were to be returned—they went away—I heard nothing more about them for three or four days—I then went to Edward's place of business—he was not there—I then met him, and he said he would go to Madell's and see about him—I expressed myself surprised at their not being returned—I afterwards saw Madell at No. 2, Wellington-street—I went to took after my chairs—I told him I insisted on having them back directly—he said he would go to the gentleman, who was a customer, at Knightsbridge, and get them that evening—I afterwards met Edwards, and I said it was not a debt, and I should take proceedings—shortly Madell called, and left two of the chairs—I have not got the other chain, nor do I know where it is—I spoke to Edwards about that—I asked him if he knew what sort of a character Madell was, and if he had heard of some proceeding going on—he said yes, and he was much astonished, for had know him some time, and thought him a respectable man, and that he should have nothing more to do with him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did Edwards afterwards agree to pay you 2 s. 6 d. a week for the goods? A. Yes, and he left one half-crown—I had known Edwards before for two or three years—he is a watch-colourer and glider—I have an I O U of his, by which he agreed to pay half-a-crown a week for the amount of the chain.
Madell. I returned the two best chains to you. Witness. Yes—the one not returned was worth 3 l. 17 s.
FREDERICK CLEMENT ELLETT . I am the son of Samuel Ellett—I first saw the prisoners on the 17th of April—Madell came to my father's shop—he wanted some dressing-cases—I went with him to the public-house—he said his customer, Mr. Whiffin, was at the public-house over the way, and I went to see him—Edwards was the only person he showed them to in the public-house—I was introduced to Edwards—I supposed him to be Whiffin—when Madell go in he began to talk to Edwards, and showed him the dressing-cases—they talked together some time, and they went out together—Madell came back and brought this paper—he said, "I want five dressingcases; I will go to your father and ask what time he can have them ready"—we made up this order—I went at nine o'clock, and when I got there Madell was not there—Edwards was there alone—I said, "Is Mr. Madell here?"—he said, "No, he is gone down to your house; he left word that you were to go back directly—you will find him at your house"—I went back, and he had not been—I then went out with my father, and met Edwards is✗ Aldersgate-street—Madell said Mr. Whiffin was brother to Miss Whiffin, and that she was principal in the business.
DAVID ABRAHAM . I took Edwards—he said his name was Mr. Whiffin, and he was an agent at Chelmsford.
JOHN HARVEY ( police-constable G 118.) I found these two bills and this letter in Madell's pocket-book.
CHARLES GOODMAN . This letter is Madell's writing, and these two bills likewise.
RHODES MOULD . I am clerk at Clerkenwell Police-court—when Mr. Ellett was making his statement, Madell interrupted, and said, "I don't Know Mr. Whiffin; there was no Mr. Whiffin mentioned;" and then he said, "I served my time to Edwards"—Edwards said, "When he met me he asked if my name was Whiffin—the name was strange to me—he asked me if I had seen some boxes—I said, "Yes, and very pretty they were."
RICHARD JOHN COVENTRY re-examined. Q. When was it you got the I O U? A. I do not remember—it was some time after this transactions took place.
Madell. I am no way connected with the chains; I know nothing about them; I had three of Mr. Coventry to show to a gentleman, one of them at 3 l. 17 s. was kept; I said I would pay for it in a few weeks; the others I returned; since then I find Mr. Edwards has agreed to pay it himself; there was 2 l. 15 s. due to me from Mr. Edwards.
COURT to RICHARD JOHN COVENTRY. Q. When was it you had the conversation with Edwards about the character of Madell? A. Within a fortnight of the 6th of March—it was certainly less than a fortnight when I had the I O U.
MADELL — GUILTY . Aged 33.—[ See page 177.]
EDWARDS — GUILTY . Aged 47.— Confined Six Months.

1278. WILLIAM LEONARD was indicted for a common assault.
ELIZABETH LEOANARD . My daughter, Elizabeth, lives with me—the prisoner is my son—he did not live with me, he used to come sometimes to see me—he came on Sunday, the 11th April—he used always to behave very kindly to me when he was sober, but when he is drunk he is very outrageous at times—he does not often get drunk—he was sober when he came, but went out, and at a quarter to six came back drunk—I and my daughter were there drinking tea—I got up to let him sit down—he said I did not act as a mother to get up to let her child sit down, that I ought to let him get a chair himself—he asked me if I would take anything to drink, and said he would fetch me something—I said I had much better not, but if he would give me a pint of beer for my supper I would much rather—he seemed very angry at that, and upset my table with the tea-things on it—I took up the poker, and said if he did not go out of my place I would make him—I did that because, if he sees me frightened when he is tipsy, it makes him think worse of it—I did not do anything with the poker, but said I would use it if he did not leave the room—I saw a knife in his hand during the transaction—I do not think that was before I caught hold of the poker, but I was so agitated at the time that I do not Know; but when I saw it, I laid hold of his arm, and told him to drop the knife—I cut my thumb and finger very slightly—they bled, but not
very much—it bled by the heat and the passion I was in—I did not put anything to it—I think he took the knife before I took the poker—after the cut with the knife I took the poker, and he laid hold of my arms behind me—I did not use it, he prevented me—he had hold of me, and I had no power of doing anything—I hit him with my head and made his mouth and nose bleed, and then he gave me some blows on my head, and about my ear—I do not know whether I fell or whether he knocked me down—I was very ill with influenza at the time—my girl called a policeman, and I was taken to the hospital, and was there a fortnight.
ELIZABETH LEONARD . I am the prosecutrix's daughter, and sister to the prisoner. I remember the prisoner coming in drunk on Sunday evening, at a quarter past six o'clock—I was taking tea with my mother—she asked him to sit down—he did not sit down—he said, "If you were like a mother you would let me get a chair myself"—he asked if she would have anything to drink—she said she would rather have a pint of beer for her supper—he got very angry, and said, "How would you look if I were to upset the table?"—she said, "Don't do that, have a cup of tea"—he upset the table—there was a knife laying there—he had it in his band—he went towards the fire-place with it, where my mother was—he went towards her—he did not do anything, he only went towards her—my mother got it out of his hand—this is my mark to my deposition—it was read over to me—( read —"The prisoner took up the knife, went towards her, and made an attempt to stab her with the knife in the chest")—I did say that my mother saved herself and got a cut—the prisoner left the room, and returned, my mother took up the poker, and said if he did not go she would use it—he caught hold of her hand, and she did not use it—he struck her, and she struck him with her head and made his nose bleed—he knocked her down.
EDWARD VOKIN ( policeman.) On the 11th of April I was called to the house, and found Mr. Leonard standing, leaning near the banisters, bleeding from the nose and mouth, and the prisoner standing alongside of her, bleeding too—I asked her who had been beating her; she said her son—I asked him what made him do it, he said, it was a family affair,—he was drunk.
Prisoner. I stated to you that I had been struck in the face. Witness. Not a word about it.
BEMERSIDE HAYWARD . I am house-surgeon at St. Bartholomew's Hospital. On the 11th of April Mr. Leonard was brought there—I examined her—she appeared very much injured—there was a swelling on the side of her head, on her wrist, and the whole side of her body—it appeared to be from repeated blows—there had also been blows on the back of the head—I considered her in danger for a short time—she had inflammation in the side, being in a precarious state of health.
Prisoner's Defence. I have been with my mother nine or ten years, and did all for her that laid in my power; I sat down to breakfast with her on Sunday morning, afterwards went out and met a friend, and remained with him till about half-past five o'clock; I went home, a few words ensued; I was struck in the face, and having had a little drop of drink, I was provoked to do it; if I had not had the drink I should have had command over my temper; I am sincerely sorry for what has happened, I have been a teetotaler some time.
ELIZABETH LEONARD , Jun., re-examined. He was not in the habit of getting drunk lately—when he was sober he behaved well to his mother—he had not lived with us for two or three weeks—at this time there was only
my mother and myself at home—the prisoner is a printer, and when he was living with us, he used to bring some of his wages to his mother.
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury, having been six weeks in prison — Confined One Month.

1281. JOSEPH BRUNSDEN was indicted for embezzlement.
WILLIAM CREED PILCHER . I keep a chandler's-shop in St. Helen's-place, Clerkenwell—the prisoner was my errand-boy—it was his duty to receive money for me—if he received this 2 s. 9 1/2 d., and 2 s. 6 3/4 d., he did not pay those sums to me, nor say any thing about them.
Cross-examined by Mr. ROBINSON. Q. When did you give him into custody? A. On the 1st of May—the second of these payments was on the 24th of April—he was in a school, and at his leisure time he attended to me—I did not pay him wages—I used to give him something occasionally—I employed him to get this money, and I had done so before—if any one paid him, he was to bring the money to me—it is about three years and a half ago since I first took him—he has received a great deal of money, at different times—I have a wife; if he had paid this money to her, it would have been right; she was not in the shop on the evening of the 24th of April—I cannot say whether she was there on the 17th of April.
SUSAN HOWELL . On the 17th of April, I paid the prisoner 2 s. 9 1/2 d. for his master, and on the 24th of April, 2 s. 6 3/4 d.—my bills came receipted, and he gave them to me.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470510-1282]
1282. JOHN CLARKE was indicted for stealing 129 sovereigns, and 55 half-sovereigns; the monies of Sarah Truman.
MR. HUDDLESTON conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH TRUMAN . I live in Kepple-street, and keep a beer-shop—I have been living with the prisoner for some years as his wife—before I lived with him, I had about 200 l. in my possession—I paid 40 l. of it to go into the house which I live in—I had got this money at various times in service, and living as housekeeper with Mr. Scott—the prisoner did not know I was possessed✗
of this money—on the 6th of May, I went out for the whole day—I returned home about a quarter-past twelve o'clock in the evening—the prisoner was not at home—he came home at past two o'clock—on the afternoon of the next day, I missed his hat-box, and then his coat and all his clothes—I then looked at my box, and found it had been broken open—I missed a green purse, containing money—I asked the prisoner if he had taken away his clothes himself—he said, he had not, but Mr. Parker (who lodged in the house) had taken out a bundle last night, at six o'clock—I gave him in charge, for✗ stealing my purse—I went with him to the police-station—the inspector refused to take the charge—I was desired to go home, and look the place over—I missed another bag, containing money, when I came home—I missed altogether, more than one hundred sovereigns, and some half-sovereigns—I gave the prisoner in charge again—I went on the Saturday to Montpelier-row, and found the whole of my property, 156 l. in sovereigns and half-sovereigns
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRAEN. Q. You did not know till you found it, how much had lost? A. I knew by what I had taken from it, what I had left—I have been living with the prisoner between four and five years—I have kept the beer-shop about three years—the prisoner is a labourerhe works at one thing or another—he was employed in a cow-shed when I first knew him—he had 18 s. a-week—he did not bring his wages home—what he brought home was very little—it went in support of house—he worked, at times, after the beer-house was taken—after he came from work he might have attended a little to the business—he never paid money, he might take have money—I had lodgers in the house—I sometimes received 7 s.✗ or 8 s. a-week from them, sometimes less—I could not save any of that—I took some from the money I had saved—I never added any to it for four years—I could not tell to a sovereign or two what I had there—the money was in the purse four years ago—this bag is one that I have had about two years—I look some money from one, and put it into the other—on the Friday, after the charge was dismissed, he held up a bag tome and said, "I have got your money"—he said, "You had a spree last night, I will have one tonight"—I had no means of living except what I took from the lodgers, and the proceeds of the beer-shop—I paid 27 l. a-year rent, and the license was 3 l. 6 s. a-year—the prisoner paid the license—I gave him the money to pay it with—I paid the rent—the receipts were not in my name.
MR. HUDDLESTONE. Q. You say when he came back he held up a bag to you, and said he had got your money? A. Yes; that was after he had been to the inspector and had the charge dismissed—in the morning I had said to him, "Will you have the kindness to clean the pots to-day"—he said, "No; I will never clean another pot as long as I live—my name is Walker "—he said, "I have got plenty of money, I am going to enjoy myself"—that was before I had missed the money—when I charged him with taking my money, he said he knew nothing at all of it, nor yet of his clothes—he said he did not know I was possessed of a farthing, and when he came back from the inspector he pulled out a small bag in which he carried his own money, and thrust it at me and said, "There is your b——y money, you got it out of b——y old Scott"—I had all this money before I went to live with the prisoner.
COURT. Q. Do you swear positively that you had all these sovereigns before you lived with the prisoner? A. I do; and none of them were got in our joint concern after we were living together.
GEORGE HERBERT . I am a cab man. I was with my cab at the Admiral Keppel stand on the night of Thursday, 6th May—the prisoner came to me From twelve to half-past twelve o'clock at night; he got into the cab, and I drove
him to Montpelier-row, Hammersmith—I took a hat-box and a bundle there—I brought the prisoner back to Collage-street, Chelsea—he asked me what my fare was, I said 4 s.—he paid me, and he said, "Here is 6 d. more, if you know nothing"—that was 6 d. over my fare—I afterwards saw the same bundle opened, and there were 156 sovereigns in it.
Cross-examined. Q. What house did you leave it in? A. In the middle house in Montpelier-row, on the right hand—I did not know the prisoner before.
JOHN MURRAY ( police-constable B 223.) I was present at the opening of the bundle—William Henry Edwards lives in the house, in Montpelier-row—he told me he was a basket-marker.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined Six months.
[trialAccount t18470510-1283]
1283. ELLEN MUNICAN and ELLEN SULLIVAN were indicted for stealing 6 blankets, value 3 l.; 8 sheets, 1 l. 1 rug, 12 s.; and 1 pair of drawers, 6 s.; the goods of William Brown, their master; and that Sullivan had been before convicted of felony.
WILLIAM BROWN . I live in Stratton-terrace, Shadwell. The two prisoners were in my service—Munican left me on Saturday, the 10th April, and Sullivan left on Monday the 12th—when she had left, I missed the articles stated—I had Munican taken up—she said she took two blankets and pawned them in one shop, and one blanket at another shop, and had shared the money with Sullivan.
MARY ANN SYRELL . I live servant with Mr. Brown. On Saturday, the 10th April, Sullivan took a blanket off the bed—she took it into the back room, folded it up, and gave it to Munican, at the bottom of the stairs—I saw her do it, and Munican directly afterwards took it out.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you? A. up stairs, in the sane room—it was taken before me—it is a lodging-house for sailors—there are not many persons coming in and out.
SAMUEL SMITH . I am a pawnbroker. I have two duplicates of a blanket pawned for half-a-crown, in the name of Ann Brown—the person who pawned it had one duplicate and I had the other—it was pawned on Saturday the 10th of April, and taken out on the Monday—I cannot say it was by either of the prisoners.
EBENEZER CLARK . I am assistant to Mr. Hall, a pawnbroker, in High-street—I have the duplicate of a pair of blankets pawned by a female, but redeemed again.
WILLIAM CHARLES POTTER ( police-constable K 212.) I went to Wapping workhouse, to apprehend Munican—I told her I wanted her, I wanted her, for robbing her master of some blankets and sheets—she said, "I am very sorry I did take the blankets and sheets"—I then went to Sullivan, I told her wanted her for taking a sheet and blanket—she said she did not, but she had received 1 s. 3 d., part of half-a-crown which Munican pawned a blanket for.
HENRY M'KAY ( police constable K 54.) I produce a certificate of Sullivan's former conviction—( read—Convicted the 23rd March, 1847, and confined ten days, solitary)—she is the person.

[trialAccount t18470510-1284]
1284. ABRAHAM BERLYN was indicted for stealing 15 pairs of shoes value 1 l. 8 s. 9 d.; the goods of Abraham Solomon, his master.
ABRAHAM SOLOMON ( through an interpreter.) The prisoner was in my ✗ vice. On the 18th of March he took out fifteen pairs of shoes—he said he sold them to Mr. Jones—he did not give me the money—he made me enter them✗ in the book to Mr. Jones, fifteen pairs of shoes, at 15 s. a-dozen, 1 l. 3 s. 9 d.—he had leave to sell shoes.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. How long have you been in England? A. Eighteen months—I have been in business about twelve months in Goulston-square, Whitechapel—I am a native of France—I know Ments—I went honourably, there and came away honourably—my wife did not come to this country with me—she lived in France till I had lived here about eight months, when I wrote to her to come—I did not leave her there in any business—I gave up business when I left—I know nothing about her being put into prison for concealing my effects—I was oncei n partnership with a man named Altham—I have not been to Mr. Hart, the beadle of the Great Synagogue, and asked him for 9 l. to keep away from this prosecution—Mr. Hart has been to my house and inquired what the prisoner had dose, and I told him all the circumstances about it—I can swear I did not ask for 9 l. to keep away from this prosecution—I did not go to the Rev. Dr. Alder and ask him for money—he sent his servant for me; I went, and heard what he wanted of me—the prisoner told me he had been a wholesale shoemaker on his own account—I did not agree with him that he was to bring me his connexion—he was to find a connexion—we were not to divide the profits—he had 25 s. a-week—I have his receipts for that—he was not to receive 1 l. a-week and one quarter of the profits—he was not to have any✗ portion of the profits—he has complained to me about the way in which I keep my books.
JAMES JONES . I sometimes purchase things of the prisoner—I did not purchase any shoes of him on the 18th of March.
Cross-examined. Q. You have purchased things of the prisoner? A. Yes Abel Solomon was known to me, through the prisoner—the prisoner was the person I dealt with, and I paid him on delivery.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor — Confined Six Months.
ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1286. AGNES FITZGERLAND was indicted for stealing 1 seedling plant called a camellia-plant, value 2 l.; 2 plants, called chrysanthams, 1 s.; and flower-pots, 2 d.; the goods of Alexander Protheroe.
ALEXANDER PROTHERON . I live at Leytonstone. I had some plants safe on Sunday night, the 25th of April—one was in my green-house, two
were in pots out of doors, and several of them were planted in my garden—on the 26th of April I was called by the policeman, and I missed them—the greater part of them are here—I can swear✗to them as being mine.
WILLIAM POTTER ( police-constable K 273.) On the morning of the 26th of April I saw the prisoner against Mr. Protheroe's fence—the gate was wide open—she was trying to conceal some of these plants under her clothes—she had got this camelia in her hand—these are the plants she had—one of these had been in the green-house, and she had it with her out of the green-house.
GUILTY .* Aged 50.— Confined Six Months.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18470510-1287]
1287. JAMES WHITE was indicated for stealing 30lbs. weight of iron, value 4 s., the goods of the Eastern Countries Railway Company.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
JOSEPH BENTON ( Police-constable K 381.) I am stationed at Stratford. In Feb. I saw the prisoner and Henry Revel, coming up the Eastern Counties Railway—I watched them—they came to a wall five of six feet high, and Revel got over—White lifted a bag up, and Revel took it from him—they went into Stratford, and carried the bag—I watched them—they saw me, and tried to make away—I ran and stopped Revel, and took the bag from him—White made his escape—this is the bag—I am sure it is the same that White gave him—it contains iron bolts and pins—the prisoner had no business on the railway, nor to get over the wall—they could go a different way.
Prisoner. Q. Where was it you saw us? A. I was against a school, at the end of Great North-street—I watched them come up the line—when Revel got over, and White handed the bag, I walked up to them—they saw me and cut up Stratford—I stopped Revel, who has been tried, and White ran as hard as he could.
JAMES GALE . I am gate-keeper in the employ of the Eastern Counties Railway Company. I can identify these bolts and nuts as the property of the railway company—here is the Eastern Counties Railway mark on them—I am in the habit of receiving them at my gate—there is such property on the railway—they are using on the line daily—people watch the workmen away to their meals, and take these things—these are as good as ever they were.
Prisoner's Defence. The officer did not see me with Revel; I did not hand the bag; I was not on the line; Revel said he should like to do something to get a month.
GUILTY .* Aged 19.— Confined Two Months.
KENT CASES.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18470510-1288]
1288. MARY ANN GILLHAM and CHARLOTTE JACKSON were indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 1 l.; 1 shawl, 19 s.; 9 yards of printed cotton, 4 s.; 1 pocket-book, 2 s.; and 1 box, 2 d.; the goods of Thomas Bakewell; and that Jackson had been before convicted of felony; to which
GILLHAM pleaded GUILTY — Confined Twelve Months.
THOMAS BAKEWELL . I am servant to Mr. James Hunt, of Sydenham. On the 4th of May, abont three o'clock, I missed from a drawer in my bed-room a shawl, a gown-piece, a watch, and a pocket-book—they had been safe at half-past two the same day—in consequence of information, I sent for a policeman, and showed me a gown-piece and shawl which I had lost—the watch and pocket-book produced are my property, and were in my drawers—I know this box.
THOMAS CAMPBELL ( police-constable R 146.) In consequence of information, on the 4th of May, I went to Deptford, and traced both prisoners to a pawnbroker's—I went into the shop, and Gillham pledging the shawl—I took it from her—Jackson was in the next box—I saw her coming out with a small basket—I asked her if it was her property—she said, "yes"—it contained a small box—she said it was her own—the gown-piece was given me by the shopman in the prisoner's presence—he said Jackson had brought it there, and left it on the counter
SARAH AVELEIGH . I am the wife of a policeman—I live at the station, and search female prisoners. On the 4th of May the prisoners were brought in—I searched Jackson, and found a watch, rolled up in a handkerchief, in her pocket—I took it from her—she said that was the worst job of all.
JAMES PHIPPS . I am a coal-dealer. On the 4th of May, at half-past two or three o'clock, I went with some coals to Mr. Hunt's, Bakewell's master—I saw both prisoners about a hundred yards from the gate, sitting on the side of the road, talking together—I saw Gillham open the gate, and go in—Jackson walked about a hundred yards further, and stopped till Gillham came out—I saw her come out—they went down the road together.
( The prisoner Jackson put in a written defence, stating, that she went of with Gillham, who proposed that they should take anything they could find at the first house they came to; that she refused to join her in any such thing; that Gillham promised she would not do so, and then left her, and went to the house; that she did not wait for her, but walked on, and Gillham came to her with a bundle.)
JOSIAH VINCENT ( police-constable R 16.) I produce a certificate pf Jackson's former conviction—( read—Convicted Aug., 1844, and confined three months)—I was present at the trial—she is the person—she has been in custody two or three times since.
JACKSON — GUILTY .— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1291. JOSEPH BARTON and THOMAS GARLICK were indicted for stealing 7lbs. weight of mutton, value 6 s.; the goods of Frederick Reeve.
JOHN SAFFREY . I am in the service of Frederick Reeve, a butcher, at Greenwich—on the evening of the 23rd of April, about nine o'clock—I concealed myself in a garden opposite the shop, and saw the two prisoners walk past the fence on the opposite side to our shop—they walked up the hill and down again—then separated, and Garlick went down a turning—Barton went across to our shop, unhooked a leg of mutton, put it under his coat, and walked away—I walked out of the gate and took him—he denied that he had it—I took it from him—I do not know where Garlick was then.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. How long had you been watching? A. An hour and a-half—it was quite dark.
JURY. Q. There was a light in the shop? A. Yes, that enabled me to see what took place.
JOHN SWAINE ( police-constable R 325.) I was on duty, and saw the two prisoner together at the top of Royal-hill—I went down my beat, and in about a quarter of an hour I was called to take Barton, who was in custody of the witness, and the meat.
BARTON — GUILTY. Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy.
Confined Three Days.
GARLICK — NOT GUILTY .
