[trialAccount t18470510-1294]
1294. JOHN GLOVER was indicted for assaulting Henry Wadlow, a police-constable, in the execution of his duty.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DAWSON conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM THORN . I conduct the business of the Crown and Anchor Tavern, at Woolwich—on Friday, the 9th of April the prisoner and Dybell came into the house three times—they behaved very abruptly indeed, abused the people at bar, and very much "annoyed" the business—about five minutes after eleven o'clock at night we refused them any liquor, and they were going into the tap-room—I called the policeman to turn them out—he said he could not come into the house unless he saw an assault made—I
then desired the prisoner to walk out—he would not—he struck me and threw me against the wall in the passage—the policeman then came in and took the prisoner—Dybell then served me in the same way-the policeman took the prisoner out of doors—I did not see what happened to the policeman—Dybell gave bail, and has not surrendered.
HENRY WADLOW ( police-constable R 267.) About eleven o'clock at night on the 9th of April, I was called to the Crown and Anchor—I heard there were disorderly persons inside, but I did not think it my duty, according to our instructions, to go inside—I then saw Mr. Thorn attempt to put the prisoner out—the prisoner took him by the collar and threw him against the wall—he then followed him, took hold of him again, and threw him on the table—I said I should take him into custody—he was very obstinate, but I succeeded in getting him outside—the moment I got him outside he hit me with his clenched fist in the eye, and knocked me down backwards, and commenced kicking me while I was down—he thrust his fingers into my throat and pressed my head to the ground—he very much hurt my throat—I had two black eyes—he fell on me with his knees on my cheat while I was down—I got on my knees and struck him with my staff—he knocked me down a second time and hit me in the eye—Dybell then came and kicked me in the side twice, while I was down—he then caught me by the thumb and pulled it back and very much injured it—I called for assistance while I was on my knees—I lost my senses, and when I came to my senses again the prisoner and Dybell were on me—I still had them by the collar—two civilians came and sprang my rattle—the prisoner was taken on the spot-Dybell made his escape, and was taken the next morning—I was unable to attend to my duty for five days, in consequence of the injuries I received—I was obliged to have medical attendance—I have been since put on lighter duty.
Prisoner. You were standing at the bar drinking—you had a pint of half-and-half. Witness. No, it is quite false.
Prisoner. It is true—I went in the tap-room and a man asked me to drink—the officer came and told me to go out—I said "I shan't till I have drank this pint and had another pint," and he struck me with his staff.
JAMES SULLIVAN . I am in the service of an eating-house keeper near the Crown and Anchor—about eleven o'clock that evening I heard the policeman crying out for assistance—I went out and saw the prisoner hit him in the eye with his hand—I ran up to lay hold of the prisoner, and he hit me between the two eyes—I got up again, and saw Dybell run up and kick the officer in the side, or in the back, while he was down—assistance came, and the prisoner was taken—Dybell got away.
HENRY EDWARD DRINKWATER . I am a surgeon—I act during the absence of Mr. Stewart, the surgeon to the Police-force—I was called to attend Henry Wadlow—he had two contusions on his forehead—his right hand was considerably bruised and lacerated—the skin of the knuckles was torn off—he appeared like a man who had been severely beaten—he suffered in consequence of it for several days.
Prisoner. I never kicked him.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18470510-1295]
1295. GEORGE SMITH was indicted for staling 1 copper goose neck of a fire-engine hose, value 8 s.; the goods of our Lady the Queen.
MESSRS. GODSON and DAWSON conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY COOK . I am a corporal of Marines, on board the Growler—the crew of that vessel are now on board the Salsette hulk, at Woolwich, while the Growler is under repairs—the prisoner was a marine on board the Growler —on the 26th April in the evening, he had leave to go on shore, and I observed a bundle under his arm—I asked him what he had there—he said, "Nothing"—I said, "Let me look and see what it is"—I found in the bundle this piece of copper pipe, "goose neck" I believe it is called—it was in a shirt—here is the mark of he board arrow on it—I took the prisoner aft, and gave him in charge under the sentinel—the prisoner had gone away in a boat to work at the yard that day.
Prisoner. Q. Was the bundle tied? A. Yes, and you untied it.
Prisoner. I found this goose neck as I was washing myself; I said to my mate, "I will take it on deck;" I did so, and the Corporal said, "What have you got there?" I said, "Some dirty things;" he would not give me time to speak, but took me; it was not my intention to take it on shore.
Witness. You were half way down the ladder before I called you back—there was a boat to take you on shore.
THOMAS WELLS . I am master of her Majesty's steam vessel the GROWLER —the prisoner was stopped by Cook—it was reported to me, and I went on deck—I asked the prisoner where he got the article from—he said he picked it up on the lower deck of the vessel—it belongs to the dock-yard where the prisoner had been employed that day.
JOHN HARWOOD . I have charge of the fire-engines in Woolwich Dock-yard—this piece of copper pipe was brought to me by the police-constable—I examined the hoses of the fire-engines in the yard—I found a goose neck missing from the store-house engine—this goose neck will fit it—the engines are made with a universal screw, it will fit any one—there was one missing, and this would fit it—it is marked with the broad arrow, and D. Y. DAVID ROY. I am clerk in the dock-yard, at Woolwich—this is part of her Majesty's stores.
BENJAMIN FISHER ( police-man R 272.) I am stationed at Woolwich—I was called on 26th April, about a quarter past seven o'clock in the evening, to take the prisoner in charge—I asked him where he got this from—he said he found it on the lower deck—I took him to the dock-yard gate—he was there charged—on conveying him to the station I said, "You are a foolish fellow"—he said he knew he was guilty, he could not help it—this is the goose neck.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 32.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18470510-1296]
1296. JAMES ELLIOTT was indicted for unlawfully having in his possession certain naval stores marked with the board arrow.
MESSRS. GODSON and DAWSON conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES BURTON ( police-constable M 272.) I was in Holland-street, near Blackfriars-bridge, on 24th of April, about nine o'clock in the evening—I was in plain cloths in company with Wild, another officer—I saw the prisoner carrying a bundle, which appeared to be heavy—I followed him to Mr. Kayser's shop in Great Guildford-street—he went into the front parlour—I
looked through the window shutters, and saw him take the handkerchief of the bundle—it was then tied in a white canvas bag—we then missed them from the window—they went into the back shop—we knocked at the door, and went in—I got a light, and saw the prisoner standing by the side of the anvil, in the back shop, with the bundle which I had seen before—I asked him what he had got there—he said he used to keep a shop at Westminster, and he worked at Wollwich, and brought this from the dock-yard—I had not at that time examined what was in the bundle—we then untied it, and found in it twenty-four bars of steel—I told him he must go to the station—in going down Guildford-street he begged of me to let him go, and said it was the first time he had ever brought anything from the dock-yard—he attempted to escape—I at last got him to the station—I searched him, and found on him three keys—the inspector asked him where he lived—he said at No. 16, Coleman-street, Woolwich—I went there, and one of the keys I found on the prisoner opened a kind of shed in the back garden, at a short distance from the house—in that we found a forge, and some other things, these five files and two copper bolts—I then searched a kind of closet, which is partly under the kitchen of the house, which I opened with another key which I found on the prisoner—we found in that closet a great quantity of steel which was new, but had no mark on it—there was 665lbs. weight of steel in that closet—I cannot say whether these two pieces of steel came from that closet or from the shed—this other piece is one of the twenty-four that were found on the prisoner at Kayser's—it is marked with the broad arrow—the steel in the closet was marked "Crowley, Millington,"
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Is this the first you have stated about the twenty-four pieces? A. No; it was said by me, before Mr. Trail, at Woolwich—the depositions were taken at Greenwich—I cannot say whether it was stated when the depositions were taken—we had two examinations.—I do not know whether I stated it at the time I signed my deposition—no one had put me on looking after the prisoner—I had received no information to look for him—I have not brought the whole that we found, only what was marked with the broad arrow—this weighs about 30lbs.—the prisoner attempted to run away as I was taking him to the station—that was after he told me he came from Woolwich, and worked at the dock-yard—my brother officer was a little before me with the 6lbs. weigh of steel on his back, which was in the prisoner's bundle—I think he saw the prisoner run away—the place we went to at Woolwich was a small shed in a garden—it had a small forge in it—there was no appearance of a smith's-yard—it is a fruit garden—I believe the prisoner has some lodgers in his house—he said at the station, in my hearing, that he brought most of this from Westminister to Woolwich, some years ago, and that it had been in his possession some years—he did not give me his address while he was in Kayser's shop—he might give it my brother officer—I was searching the back part of the shop—I believe there was gas in the front—I could see the bundle in the window—I could not see when I went in—the back shop was dark.
MR. GODSON. Q. Was there a forge in the shed, large enough to wars iron, and take the mark out? A. Yes.
GEORGE WILD ( police-constable M 94.) I was in company with Burto✗ on the 24th of April—I saw the prisoner going along Holland-street, with a bundle tied inn a check-handkerchief—we followed him to No. 27, Great Guildford-street—he knocked, and was admitted—we waited outside, against.
the shutters of the front room on the ground-floor—we saw the prisoner inside, and the bundle was standing on a form about two feet high—it remained there some time, while the prisoner and the person in the shop were looking at some tools—there were some holes cut in the shutters, through which we looked, and saw them—Kayser's wife then went out, and we missed the prisoner and another party from the room—we knocked, and a female came—we asked if Mr. Kayser was there, and said we wanted to speak to him—he came and admitted us—we went in, and found the prisoner in the back room, standing against the anvil—I asked Kayser where the parcel or bundle was that that gentleman had brought in—I looked, and it stood on the anvil—I asked the prisoner where he worked—he said, at Woolwich Dock-yard—I said, "Where did you get this"—he said, he brought it from Woolwich Dock-yard—I said, "It is property you are not allowed to bring away by any means; you must consider yourself in custody; we are two police constables"—I took the steel, and Burton took charge of the prisoner—he said he was a respectable person, he had been in business, and it was a portion of his property when he was in business at Brewer's green, Westminster—I was a little in advance of Burton and the prisoner—I looked round and missed them—I went to the top of the street, and hallooed out, "Burton, have you got the prisoner?"—he said, "Yes, I have"—I said, "Hold him till I come"—the prisoner was taken to the station—a watch, and three keys, and 17 s. in money, were found on him—he was asked where he lived—he said he rented a house at No. 16, Coleman-street, and was a respectable man—I went to that house, and with one of the keys found on the prisoner, Burton unlocked a shed, where we found a quantity of steel and tools, and these files—there was some steel piled up—I put my hand behind it, and found these copper bolts—I examined a closet-down under the kitchen—it was opened by another of the keys found on the prisoner—we went down five or six steps, and under part of the kitchen, we found in the closet an immense quantity of steel of the same kind as this, and such tools as stonemasons use—these files were found in the shed where we found the copper bolts—part of the steel in the shed had the broad arrow on it—the steel found in the closet was of the same kind.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there any part of this man's premises that you did not rummage? A. I should say there was—we did not interfere with the bed-room—we went through the kitchen, but did not examine it very minutely—we have brought five files, two or three pieces of copper, and two or three pieces of steel, which have got the broad arrow on them—the prisoner told us in the shop, that he lived at No. 16, Coleman-street, Woolwich—I then walked away—the prisoner and Burton were close to my heels at first—I then went to Burton's assistance, to catch the man who had given his address—I never had an Admiralty case before, nor a Mint case, or Custtoms—I do not know what the Government allowance is—I do not know what I shall get—the prisoner said he had most of the steel when he was in business in Brewer's-green, Westminster, and that he took it to Woolwich—he said he was going to sell part of the steel he had at home—he did not tell me he was going to move—he afterwards said he bought the steel, and he could satisfy us where he bought it, but the person I think he said was gone abroad—he did not mention Mr. Branscomb, or Bedford, or Winter, or Rich—he never said a word to me whom he bought it of, but since he has been in custody his wife said something about bills.
MR. GODSON. Q. When did he say he bought it of a person that was
gone abroad? A. The same night, before he was charged—he said he could satisfy us he bought it, but the person was gone abroad—he said that about half-past nine o'clock, before the inspector took the charge—he first mentioned about Westminster, in Kayser's shop, after he said he came from Woolwich.
DAVID ROY . I am clerk in the store-keeper's office, in Woolwich Dock-yard. I know the prisoner—he has been employed in the steam-factory there—he was so in April—this copper bolt is the property of the Crown—bolts of this sort are never sold—here are three broad arrows on it—this piece of steel has the mark on it—on these files the broad arrow is obliterated—the maker's name is on them—I cannot distinguish the broad arrow on any of them, but I have no doubt it has been there—here is the place where it has been.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know anything of the prisoner beyond paying him his wages? A. I do not pay him—I know he was a mechanic in the Dock-yard—I believe he is a mechanic in his own yard—I saw a shed on his premises, and he had a forge—I know nothing of his being employed by his fellow-workmen in repairing their tools—the workman in the dock-yard use the Government tool, not their own,
COURT. Q. Are those pieces of steel made there? A. No, we get them supplied by contract—some of them come with the broad arrow on, and some without—they are marked, "Crowley, Millington"—he is the Government maker—he is a very extensive maker—on the files are, "John Tubb" and "Turton"—they are the Government makers of these.
THOMAS WRIGHT . I am a labourer in the Dock-yard, and lodge at the prisoner's house, No. 16, Coleman-street, Woolwich—there is a garden at the back of the house, and a shed in it—I have seen the prisoner go into that shed many times—I never saw it open except when he was there—he unlocked it when he went in, and locked it each time when he came away—he left home about half-past six o'clock on the 24th of April.
Cross-examined. Q. What part of the house do you occupy? A. The lower room or kitchen—that is adjoining to the closet where these things were found—I never worked for the prisoner but on one occasion—he does work as a smith there—he is employed by his fellow-workmen a little—he works a little of a night—he repairs tools for masons.
Q. Is it fact that you have applied to the landlord to become tenant of that house in the room of this man, since his apprehension? A. I went to Mr. Sims, the landlord—I did not tell him they were going to transport Elliott on my evidence—I did not say, "I am the principal evidence against him—in the event of Elliott being transported, will you let me have the house?"—I do not know what I am to be paid for coming here to give evidence.
MR. GODSON. Q. Do you know the place where these things were found? A. Yes—there is another room parts that and mine—I never saw that place unlocked, unless Elliott unlocked it himself, and he always locked it after him.
MR. PARNELL called
JAMES SIMS . I am a builder, and live at Woolwich—I am the owner of several houses there—the prisoner has been my tenant for six years, since 1841—I have witnessed his character—one part of the yard which he occupies, I occupy for a building-yard—he carries on the business of a smith to some extent—he has been an industrious honest man—he has been at work
in the dock-yard since 1841—he has been part of that time employed by Grissel and Peto—he took the house of me in 1841—he came from some part of Westminster—I gave him notice to quit on the 25th of March, because I wanted to make some improvements in the house—I saw the witness Wright on Sunday, the 25th April—hecame to ask me whether I was aware that Elliott was taken as a prisoner—I said, "No"—I asked him what for—he stated, for having possession of Government stores—he asked me whether I would accept him as tenant for his house, and whether the shop which Elliott worked in, belonged to me—I stated that it did—he then asked me whether Elliot owed me any rent—I replied, he did—he said Elliot contemplated taking the shop down—I said, "If Elliott takes it down, I shall transport him"—he said, "No, they will save you the trouble"—I said, "I have made use of hard words, but I will not allow him to remove it."
MR. GODSON. Q. Did you not give the prisoner notice to quit your house because it was being put in a bad character, by certain women frequenting it? A. I did not—I was not aware that women frequented it, only as lodgers—I am quite sure the prisoner was my tenant for the shed—it was fastened by a padlock, which he put on about five years ago—I have seen that shed open for days together—the prisoner was employed by Grissel and Peto, doing Government work in the yard.
GEORGE KAYSER . I am a smith, and live in Guilford-street. I have known the prisoner eight years—he bore a very good character while he was employed at the Parliament-house, and as a master for himself at Brewer's green, Westminster, where he had a shop to do mason's tools—after that I did not see him for some years.
COURT. Q. How long is it since he was at Westminster? A. Seven or eight years ago—I do not know at what time he went away—the policemen came and asked where he came from—he said he worked at Woolwich Dock-yard, and lived at Woolwich—I am not certain whether he told them at what place—he said he brought these things from his house at Woolwich—he said he had worked in Westminster before, where he was in business, and he had a quantity of steel by him that he wished to dispose of, because he was going to move from his house where he was living, and he wished to give up his shop.
MR. GODSON. Q. Have you had any steel with the broad arrow on it? A. No—if I had seen any I should know it—I did not expect the prisoner—he came accidentally to me that night—I had not seen the metal till the policemen opened it—I never saw the broad arrow on it—I saw them take two or three pieces out of the bag—I did not see any mark on them—if I had bough that metal I should have worked it into mason's tools—it is as good as other steel, it is the same as what we buy—I have seen Mr. Sims now—I did not know him before—I make a great many mason's tools—I buy steel of merchants and other persons that have it to sell—I have been to Woolwich once or twice—I have been in Coleman-street—I have known where the prisoner lived about two or three months—I was down at Woolwich, about a week or nine days before the 24th of April—he had a good deal of steel in his smith's shop—I did not see any copper.
MR. PARNELL. Q. You did not think it was your business to rummage the shed and place? A. No—it is part of the business of almost every smith to purchase old steel and copper—they use it to make tools for themselves and others
CHARLES ROGERS . I am a boot-maker, and live at Brewer's-green, Westminster. I have known the prisoner thirty-seven years, in Somersetshire and in London—he lived with me at Brewer's-green—he was then a smith, as he
is now—I recollect his moving about six years ago—he had then a quantity of metal in his shop adjoining his house—I cannot say what quantity—I suppose he had several hundred weight—he has borne the character of as honest respectable man amongst his friends and neighbours—he has called on✗ me since he left me—I have never called on him.
MR. GODSON. Q. Are you any relation of his? A. No—he lived with me about two years—his business was making tools for masons—he had old and new iron rods—I used to see a great deal about—I did not take particular notice of them—he rented the upper part of my house at 20 l. a year, and had his shop adjoining—while he was at Westminster he was in the habit of making all the tools for Lee, of Westminster.
GEORGE BARNES . I live at Woolwich—I am a mason, and a contractor for the Board of Ordnance. I have known the prisoner eight years—he bore a very honest character—I never was in his shed—he is employed to make and sharpen tools there—he has sharpened picks, and axes, and other tools for me—he is in the habit of making tools of other persons.
MR. GODSON. Q. Name any other person for whom he has made tools? A. I cannot call one to mind just at present—at the time he was at Westminster he made tools for the masons that worked in the new houses of Parliament—I cannot call to mind any one for whom he has made tools at Woolwich—I am a contractor under the Board of Ordnance—they do not allow their workmen to carry on trade on their own account, that I am aware✗ of—I live about a quarter of a mile from the house where the prisoner lives, at Woolwich—I have been in his garden since he has been in custody—I went to pay his wife a bill for sharpening some tools.
MR. PARNELL. Q. Do you know Mr. Bell and Mr. Prentice? A. Yes—I know the prisoner has worked for Mr. Prentice—I am not sure whether he has for Mr. Bell—I have been employed by the Board of Ordnance—I am doing jobs for myself now.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 39.— Fined 5 l . and Imprisoned Three Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1297. EDWARD HOLTON was indicted for stealing 2 shillings the monies of Edward Batchelor:—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the monies of Philip Langford.
MATTHEW M'GUIRE . I am a Greenwich pensioner. I met the prisoner on Saturday, the 1st of May—I went to a public-house and had a pot of the ale with him—I put half-a-crown on the table—the boy brought the ale and took up the half-crown—the prisoner got up and met the boy before he came back, and asked him for the change—he said the half-crown was his own—it was not his, it was mine.
Prisoner. Q. Did you put it down on the table or did I? A. I took it out of my pocket and put it down—I swear it was mine—you did not put it on the table.
EDWARD BATCHELOR . I am servant to Mr. Philip Langford, who keeps the public-house. I took the half-crown from the table and went for the change—the prisoner came out and asked me for the change of his half-crown—I did not know whose it was—I gave him the two shillings.
Prisoner. Q. Did I put the half-crown towards you? A. No—it was lying on the table—I did not know whose it was.
GUILTY .* Aged 21.— Confined Two Months.

1300. HENRY LOCK was indicted for stealing 31lbs. of lead, value 5 s.; the goods of Henry Hunt Piper, his master; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
HENRY HUNT PIPER . I live at 42, Eastcheap—the prisoner was in my service at work at a building at Dulwich—a constable produced some edges✗ of lead to me which are mine—-the prisoner had no business with them.
JOHN HARRISON ( policeman.) On the 19th of April, about twenty minutes to seven o'clock in the evening, I met the prisoner on Camberwell-green—I saw something bulky in front of his trowsers, stopped him, and asked him what it was—he said he had got nothing—I felt something, and asked him what it was—he said it was only a piece of lead which he had found—I took him to the station—I have fitted the lead to the building belonging to the prosecutor—it fits exactly—I found 31lbs. of lead on him altogether.
HENRY HUNT PIPER re-examined. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted June, 1839, of stealing lead, and confired six months)—I was prosecutor on that occasion, and am certain he is the man.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. I believe he has been twenty years in your service? A. More—I took him from gaol into my service again—he had 21 s. a week, generally more—I had rather not say how he has behaved.
GUILTY Aged 33.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18470510-1301]
1301. WILLIAM BUCKLEY was indicted for stealing 39 pair of leather fronts for boots, value 4 l.; 96 pairs of leather half-fronts for boots, 10 l.; the goods of John Poole and another.
WILLIAM POOL . I assist my father, John Poole, who is in partnership with my brother—we live at Walworth-common. On the 8th of Feb., at eight o'clock at night, I fastened up the shop—I was called by Harris about eight o'clock at next morning, and found the place broken open, and a quantity of leather fronts stolen—I had seen them there the night before—they were worth 13 l. or 14 l.—none of them have been found.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did you offer a reward of 10 l.? A. Yes, next day.
JANE STOKES . I live at Bermondsey—I know the prisoner very well—two or three months ago, I do not know the date, he came to my house and brought a bag containing a quantity of fronts, grafts, and Clarences—I said, "I see you have got work"—he said, "Yes"—I said, "Where did you get it?"—he said, "From Mr. Batty, of Shoreditch"—I said, "I am glad of it, I hope you will keep it"—he went down stairs for five minutes, leaving the
goods at my place—I looked at the top of the bag—took up one which was at the top—it had the letter "G" on it—as nearly as I can guess from the bulk there were from seven to nine dozen—I do not know whether "G" was upon them all—I did not look at more than one—the prisoner came up stairs again—I said, "You smell of gin"—he said, "Yes, I have been drinking all the morning; I have cracked a crib, "by which I understood breaking a place open—I said, "Oh, my God! where?—he said, "Pool's—I said, "For God's sake, take them away"—I noticed a diamond on the bag, with the letters "B and S" on it—it was more dirty than the bag produced—it✗ was like a coal bag—it might have been such a bag as this, but blacker.
Cross-examined. Q. You knew what cracking a crib meant, why not go and tell the police? A. He was half tipsy, and I did not know whether it was true or not—I do not know now whether it is true or not—it is what he told me—I did not tell the police—I told the prisoner to take it away, that was all I wished—the policeman did not come and talk to me, nor did I give any information—I told my son about it, and it was him that gave information—he was not by at the time the prisoner said he had cracked a crib —he does not live with me—my husband was present, but he is deaf, he could not hear what was said—he was before the Magistrate on the first occasion, but was not examined, and on the second he had nothing to say—he is not here to day—all that he heard was the prisoner saying he had got work from Mr. Batty, of Shoreditch—he was sitting at his work about two and a half yards from the prisoner—I did not say when I was first spoken to about it, that I thought the bag had the letter "B" on it—I did not think anything about it—I said the leather had "G" on it—if anybody comes in with work we always look as it—I told him I had looked at the bag—my son was examined before the Magistrate—the police never spoke to me about this—my son spoke to me about it two or three weeks ago—I think it was this day three weeks that my son gave information about it to Mr. Pool—I and my son have not had a great deal of talk about this—I only told him about cracking the cirb —he went of his own accord to give information—I did not hear that 10 l. reward was offered till this day three weeks, when I was at the Court—my son did not tell me of it before—I did not bring him here—I saw him here this morning.
GEORGE DYER . I am in the employ of Mr. Pool. On the 13th of Feb., when I went to work in the morning, I missed about eleven dozen of work in an apron similar to this one produced—it had the same mark as this, and a diamond—this is produced to show what it was like.
Cross-examined. Q. You have not found your apron? A. No.
WILLIAM MOSS ( policeman.) I took the prisoner—I told him the charge—he said very well, he would come—Mr. Poole had sent for him—I told him the goods stolen were Clarence fronts, and some other things, and I understood he had offered some of the same description for sale to a Mr. Hoare—he stood he had offered some of the same description for sale to a Mr. Hoare—he said, "Very well; I am very much obliged to you for telling me what has been brought against me—he was told the charge again at the station, and said, "I never offered any for sale, and never had any in my possession."
Cross-examined. Q. Was Mr. Stokes, sen., examined on the first occasion? A. No—he was on the second—the son was asked a question or two—I believe there us a reward of 10 l. to the paid on the conviction to the person giving the information.
WILLIAM POOL re-examined. My boots had the letter "G" marked on them—the paper offering the reward did not say that they were in a bag marked "G."
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18470510-1302]
1302. JOSEPH SPIGNELL and ALFRED DUCKETT were indicted for stealing 1 brooch, value 4 s., the goods of Elizabeth Hurt, from her person; and that Duckett had been previously convicted of felony.
ELIZABETH HURT . I am single, and live at St. John's-place, Walworth. On the 5th of April I went to the Surrey Theatre with some friends—I left about twenty minutes to twelve o'clock, and went into the parlour of the Equestrian Tavern, by the side of the theatre—after a short time I and another person left the parlour, and passed to the bar—the prisoners were standing together at the bar, but whether in company I cannot say—when I got to the street-door two females and a man stood there—I asked them to make way for us to pass—as we passed Spignell threw a glass of beer at my friend, but it did not touch her—he made a rush at her after that—he put the glass down, and said to a woman who was with him, "Give me my key"—that woman held her head down—I was passing at the same time, and he made a rush at me as I passed—I said, "That man has stolen my brooch"—he ran out at the side-door—I gave an alarm, and ran after him—I crossed the road—Duckett caught hold of me, and asked me what I wanted with that man, what I was going to do with him—I said, "The man has stolen my brooch"—he said, "You are not going to give him in charge for the brooch?"—I said, "Certainly I am"—he said, "What do you want with him? what are you going to do with him?"—I said, "I shall give him in charge"—he then laid hold of me, and threw me between the edge of the pavement and the road—my brooch was safe on my breast when I left the parlour—I had that moment fastened it, and I felt Spignell's hand at my neck
Cross-examined by MR. NAYLOR. Q. Had you a shawl on? A. Yes—I was not at all warm at the theatre—it was rather slack—the warmth of the parlour made my friend's head ache, and she asked me to go into the street with her—I had not pulled my shawl off in the room—I left because the theatre was over—I was out of service at the time, and had been for two months—I did not hear a quarrel between one of the prisoners and a woman—he threw something over my friend, and said to the woman, "Give me my keys"—he went towards the woman to get the keys, and she held her head down—I do not know whether that was that he should not put his hand into her bosom—she was not trying to escape from him—I did not hear him swear—after that he went out of the house—it was about twelve o'clock—the woman did not say she would lock him up—I did not hear her say anything else—there was no confusion at all—I believe Spignell is an omnibus driver—there was no crowd in the theatre, it was very thin indeed—the woman did not give him the key—I followed him to the door—as I got to the door my handkerchief came undone, and I found I had lost my brooch—I had my sister with me—several people went into the tavern with us, a friend, and her husband, and Mr. Thompson—I was with him all the evening—he did not go to see us home—we had all been together that evening—my sister went to his house for him—there were seven of us altogether.
Duckett. Q. Were there many people in the house? A. I did not notice—you did nothing to attract my attention till the man threw something at us—it was you that stopped me crossing the road—I did not notice either of you till his happened—when you came up to me I was following Spignell—I had not been up the street—I had not been out of the house a moment, merely crossing the road—the policeman fetched me to see if I was the
woman who had lost the brooch, as they had got the main in custody—they had taken him while you were holding me on the opposite side of the way, opposite the public-house—I was lying in the road—you had thrown me down, it was at the corner of the Blackfriars-road.
ELIZABETH GABITASS . I am the wife of William Gabitass, of Market-street—I was with the prosecutrix about twelve o'clock that night—I left the parlour of the tavern with her—in passing the bar I saw the prisoners—the proseuctrix asked one of the women to move to let us pass—Spignell took a glass with something in it from the counter and threw it—it went partly over me and partly over the bar—he said, "Take that," put the glass down again, made a rush at somebody who stood there, and, said, "Give me the keys, or I will knock your brains out"—he struck at the person—I got out of the way—I turned round to look after my friend, and Spignell had got his hand at her neck, where her brooch was—directly she got past them to the door she complained of her loss—Spignell ran across the London-road towards the Borough-road—I went out after the prosecutrix—I crossed, and thought she was with the policeman, but she was not—I went to look after her, and saw Duckett had got hold of her round the waist—he threw her down and ran towards the tavern again, and there the policeman took him—I had seen the brooch in my friend's shawl as we left the parlour to go out not two minutes before.
Cross-examined. Q. You had been to the theatre? A. Yes—I could not get a seat, and waited about the theatre some time—I saw the performance—I was waiting about from half-past nine till the theatre was over—I was looking at the performance part of the time—I afterwards joined my friends and went to the public-house—the prisoner seemed as if he was quarrelling with the person who stood right behind me—I think he intended to throw the beer over her, but it went on me, some of it—he seemed to✗ make a blow at somebody—I did not see whether he struck her—there was a man and a female talking to her—Spignell said, "Give me the key, I will not be played with, "and threw the glass at her—I do not know whether he hit her—I did not hear her cry out—she held her head down to avoid his blow, I suppose—I did not see her afterwards—there was a great deal of confusion—the bar was very full—there were a good many people drinking there—I do not know that there were many there till the cry of "Stop thief, "and of course then they all rushed into the public-house—I had never seen the prisoners before I saw Spignell's right hand on the prosecutrix's neck—after he attempted to get the keys he ran away—I do not know whether he got anything from her—the prosecutrix said, "Stop that man, he has robbed me"—I was not alarmed till she had been robbed.
Duckett. Q. Did you see me in the house? A. I saw you standing with Spignell—I cannot say whether you were drinking with him—I afterwards saw you crossing the Blackfriars-road, laying hold of my friend, and you crossed to the tavern again—she said to the policeman, "Stop that man, he has knocked me down"—I cannot say whether that was the policeman who took you—you were taken immediately.
COURT. Q. was there any confusion before Spignell produced it by throwing something out of a glass? A. No—I do not know whether it was a real quarrel, or whether it was merely done to draw my attention off—they were not near each other.
SAMUEL CROSS ( police-constable M 83.) I was in the Borough-road, and heard a cry of "Stop thief"—I saw Spignell running with a crow after
him—I caught him, and said, "What are you running for?"—he said, "Let me go, I have only been striking a woman"—I took him back to the door of the tavern and made inquiry—I thought it was nothing but an assault—the mob rescued him form me—some one said, " Hook it," or something meaning "make off," and Spignell escaped from the public-house, through another door, into the Borough-road—I afterwards saw him in charge of another policeman, who also took Duckett—I took charge of Duckett—they were both taken to the station—the prosecutrix said Spignell had robbed her the moment the policeman took him.
Cross-examined. Q. Spignell is an omnibus or cab driver, is he not? A. A cab driver—he works for Mr. Beard—I do not recollect his saying he did not want to be locked up all night—he said, "Let me go, I have only struck a woman"—he afterwards said he should not be able to attend to his duty, and it would the ruin of his family.
Duckett. Q. Was the cry of "Stop thief" in the Blackfriars-road or the London-road? A. At the top of the Borough-road—Blackfriars-road crosses the Circus—there was another policeman on the beat near me—there was no charge made against you at the time of stopping the woman—I saw you in the custody of the other policeman at the corner of the Borough-road—he gave you into my custody about seventy yards from the public-house, over against the cab stand.
GEORGE BATSFORD ( police-constable M 70.) I heard a cry of "Stop thief!"—saw Spignell running, and stopped him—Duckett came up—I saw Spignell pass something out of his right hand into Duckett's left—I seized Duckett by the cuff of his coat—he swung his hands round to try to get away—I gave him to a policeman who came up at the time.
Cross-examined. Q. This was after Spignell had been in the custody of Croft? A. Yes.
COURT. Q. What does " Hook it " mean? A. To escape.
Duckett. Q. When you caught hold of me, what did I say to you? A. Nothing—I took hold of your right hand—I did not hear you beg me to search you—I gave you to Croft directly—if Spignell had passed anything to you I should not have seen what it was.
Prisoner Duckett to SAMUEL CROFT. Q. What did I say to you? A. That you did not know what you were taken for, and asked me to search you—you had nothing in your hand then—I took you five or ten minutes after the assault—I did not see the woman then—she came across, and said, "That is the man who took my brooch," and we went down to the station together—that was the second time I took you—I did not see anything of her the first time—Spignell was in custody when I saw her—the crowd at the public-house had called "Stop thief!"
Prisoner Duckett's Defense. The prosecutrix says she was going across the road when I knocked her down; there was no appearance of her being knocked down when she was at the police-court, or it would have been shown; I insisted upon being searched; the policeman held one of my hands; I held out the other, and said, "Is there anything in my hands?" and begged them to search me there, but they would not till I came to the station; if I had the property in my possession, should I have a skedhim to search me? is it likely, if the man ran away down the Borough-road with the brooch, that he would have carried the property, and then give it to me? the policeman says he took me directly he stopped Spignell; is it possible I could have been in the Blackfriars-road and insulted the prosecutrix, and asked her
the questions she says I did, and yet he in the Borough-road when I was stopped? when it is said Spignell ran as fast as he could all the way; expected a witness here who was in my company the whole time; I saw mob in the Borough-road, and went with him to see what it was; Spignell, who was in custody, said, "It is only a row; the policeman instantly said, "Why, you have parted with something to him;" I said, "It is no such thing;" the case is entirely got up by the policeman, on purpose that they should both have a charge.
JOSEPH HUGHFS ( police-constable E 114.) I produce a certificate of Duckett's former conviction, from Mr. Clark's office—I was present at the trial—he is the person—( read—Convicted Oct., 1845; confined nine months.)
SPIGNELL — GUILTY . Aged 35.
DUCKETT — GUILTY . Aged 17.
Transported for Ten Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18470510-1303]
1303. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing 1 piece of metal re✗bling a shilling, value 1/2 d., the property of John Thomas Willmore; and that he had been previously convicted of felony; to which he pleaded
GUILTY . — Confined Twelve Months.
Before Mr. Baron Alderson.
[trialAccount t18470510-1304]
1304. JONH HOBBS, THOMAS FREDERICK WEBSTER , and ROBERT LEWER , were indicted for feloniously setting fire to a stack of hay, the property of Frederick Piggott; and that Webster had been before convicted of felony.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
FREDRICK PIGGOTT . I am an estate agent, and have a farm in the kew-road, in the parish of Richmond. At the back of that farm I had a hay-rick sixty or seventy yards from the high-road—on the 23rd of April, at a quarter to eight o'clock n the evening, I went round it, and saw it safe—at half-past eight, or a quarter to nine, I received information, which induced me to go to it again—I found it on fire, it was in a blaze all the way round—there were some faggots under it burning—it had been laid on faggots—with the assistance of some of my neighbours, I extinguished the fire—the faggots were burnt—it is impossible that combustion could be the cause of it, because it was old hay—I know nothing of the prisoners.
HENRY UPTON ( policeman.) On the night of the 23rd of April I was on duty in the Kew-road, on the Richmond side of Mr. Piggott's hay-rick, about 200 yards from it—about five minutes after eight o'clock in the evening, and met the three prisoners going towards the premises, about 200 yards from the place—when they saw me they made a stop—two of then looked round, and crossed over the road—the rick was safe when I had passed it—in about twenty minutes I saw it on fire.
SAMUEL WINLET . I am a market-gardener, and live at Richmond. On the night of the 23rd of April, a little after half-past eight o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoners in Sandy-lane—Mr. Piggott's meadow joins the lane—they were coming as if from the hay-rick—they were n the lane when I first saw them—at the time I saw them I noticed the hay-rick—it was on fire—they had their backs towards it—they asked me if there was an engine kept in the parish—I said, yes, there was—they came towards the cross road,
towards Richmond—I went towards the rick—in going from Sandy-lane into Kew-road they would not have to cross the meadow where the stack was (not where I met them,) but in getting from Kew-road to Sandy-lane they would—I had seen them at half-past two o'clock the same afternoon walking from Sandy-lane—they asked me where that road led them to—I know nothing about them.
Prisoner Webster. Q. When we met you in the afternoon did we speak to you? A. You asked where the road led; I said, "To Richmond," and you went that way—you were in company with the other prisoners.
KINGSTON MARK ( police-sergeant.) On 23rd April I went to the place where the fire was, and afterwards went to the police-station and found the three prisoners there—I did not hold out any inducement or threat to them—they told me they wished to make a statement to me—that they had come to give themselves up for setting fire to the hay rick—I told them I was willing to hear anything they might have to say at the same time, I cautioned them that it would be taken down, and produced in evidence—I asked them if they were willing that I should take down what they said—they said, "Yes," and I did so—they were all three together—as one made a statement I asked the others if they agreed with it—they said, "Yes," and they all signed it—this is it—( produced)—I took their shoes from their feet—I saw footmarks near the stack, and compared those footmarks with their shoes in the direction they said they went, and found they corresponded.
The prosecutor's statement was here read, as follows:—"John Hobbs, a native of London, labourer; Thomas Frederick Webster, a native of London, labourer; Robert Lewer, a native of London, labourer; state, that between eleven and twelve o'clock this day, they came into Richmond by way of Kew Bridge; that they begged the halfpence to cross the bridge, and were the whole day in Richmond without anything to eat; that their lives for some time past had been spent in such misery and poverty, they were determined, when they left London this morning, to do something to alter it, and looked out for a stack to set fire to, About a quarter or half-past eight o'clock, as nearly as they can say, they climbed over the gate at the farm where the stack was set fire to (Mr. Piggott's); they all three went to the back part of the hay-stack; they then made three holes in the stack (by pulling out the hay so as to admit their arms as far as they could reach) one in the centre, the others at each end of the back part of the stack from the barn, when they all three lit lueifers from a pipe which Hobbs had, and set fire in the different holes at the same time; the side of the stack instantly was in a blaze; that they then ran away, and got through a gap of the hedge behind the stack, and then through another hedge by a gap into a ploughed-field, across this field, over a white gate, into a lane behind, (a road); then turned to the right at the top of the lane, and passed New Richmond into the main road; they then returned down the road and looked at the fire, where they stopped till the engine arrived, and then returned to Richmond, came to the police-station, where they gave themselves up. They also all declare, that they voluntarily make this statement, and that no promise has been held out to them; and that they have been properly cautioned that what they said would be taken down and produced in evidence against them.
"JOHN HOBBS ,
"THOMAS FREDRICK WEBSTER ,
"ROBERT LEWER."
Webster's Defence. My Lord and gentlemen of the jury: the offence of which I stand charged is one of a very heavy character, and which, I am very sorry to say, has brought me before your notice this day. My Lord and gentlemen, perhaps you will not credit what I am going to say to you; but I will fearlessly speak to you, convinced that I am innocent, and that what I am going to say is true. It is true that I was in Richmond during the day on which the fire occurred, and I also was the company of the other persons charged with me, and that I was also present at the fire; but I can assure your Lordship that the stack was not fired by me. But being reduced to the deepest distress, through the want of work, and not having tasted food for some time past, I was induced to accompany the other persons, and accuse myself of committing the crime, which has caused my friends a great deal of anguish and regret, that I had not applied to them for assistance. My Lord, I was never in the company of those persons accused with me, but on theday the fire occurred; nor can I, to my recollection, ever remember seeing them before. the only thing that criminates me, is the marks which the officer states was made by the shoes which I wore; but I can assure you that they were not the print of my footsteps; for I never entered the field where the ground was capable of receiving any impression. My Lord, it is not at all improbable that the marks were caused by some person who assisted at the fire, and who, in the excitement of the moment, has not since remembered crossing the field. But, my Lord, I hope you will pause before you proceed to sum up the evidence adduced against me; for it would be doing me a great injustice without being convinced of my guilt; for the confession which I signed in an unthinking moment, and which has been used as evidence against me, which the keen pangs of hungers has induced me to put my signature to, is a falsehood, of which I have had reason to repent. My lord, taking all these considerations before your notice, and weighing the evidence brought against me, I may be led to hope for a favourable issue of my trial, and which I may be led to expect from an impartial and uncorrupted jury.
WILLIAM POCOCK ( police-constable F 14.) I produce a certificate of Webster's former conviction—( read—Convicted June, 1844, and confined two✗ years)—I was present at the trial—he is the person—he has been convicted at this Court since that, and had three months—I proved the former conviction then.
WEBSTER — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Twenty Years.
HOBBS — GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
LEWER — GUILTY . Aged 16.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1306. MARGARET SHEEN and ANN SHEEN were indicted for stealing, in the dwelling-house of Edward M'Dermott, 1 ring, value 8 l.; 1 neck chain, 15 s.; 1 brooch, 10 s.; 1 medal, 6 l.; 1 seal, 1 l.; 1 cloak, 3 l.; 7 gowns, 4 l.; 2 petticoats, 7 s.; 1 sheet, 5 s.; 1 blanket, 12 s.; 1 wrapper, 12 s.; 2 towels, 1 s.; 1 pair of clogs, 6 d.; and 1 locket, 10 s.; the goods of Alfred Driver, the master of Margaret Sheen; to which
MARGARET SHEEN pleaded GUILTY . Aged 19.— confined Four Months.
ALFRED ERICER . I live at No. 63, Nelson-square, Blackfriars—the prisoner Margaret was in my service—I went out on the 11th of April, leaving her there—when I came back she was gone, and the property mentioned also—she had been with me about three months—the articles produced are mine.
SARAH BARTON . I live at No. 63, Nelson-square—Mr. Driver lodges there—I went out on the 11th of April—Margaret Sheen had the charge of the house—I returned about eight o'clock that evening; she was gone, and I believe all these articles—I had seen Ann Sheen there twice—she was there on the 11th April, but she went out before me—I saw her and Margaret go up stairs towards Mr. Driver's room, and when Ann came away she had a bundle with her—she is Margaret's sister—I had seen her there once before.
ANN MACK . Ann Sheen came to me on 11th April, and dined with me—she went out, returned with two bundles, and said her sister had made her a present of them—I did not see what they contained—I saw some silk in one of them—this is the silk, and I saw a velvet cloak—the bundles were left at my house—I gave them to the officer the next morning.
JAMES BURTON ( police-constable M 272.) I went to Mr. Mack's on the 12th April, and got these two bundles—they contain all the articles stated.
Ann Sheen's Defence. My sister came and asked me to come to her place, as she was going to leave; I went to fetch her things, not knowing there was any stolen property amongst them; she told me they were her clothes; I did not know any difference until I was taken into custody—if she will speak the truth she will tell the same.
ANN SHEEN. GUILTY.— AGED 21.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Confined Four Months.

1307. JAMES BENNETT was indicted for stealing 4 bushels of oats, value 15 s., and 1 sack, 2 s.; the good of Henry Dunkin, in a barge on the navigable river Thames.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
DANIEL DAVIES . I live in King's-row, Horsleydown. I am a watchman to a brewery. On the 20th of April, about nine o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner and a man named Cook coming up a turning leading to Horsleydown Old-stairs—I knew of a barge of Mr. Dunkin's lying there—Cook was carrying a hat and a bag, and directly after him came the prisoner with a sack—I went to take hold of him, and put my hand on the sack—he dropped it, and Cook turned back and asked what I wanted to do, and showed fight —the prisoner got away, but was stopped by some persons—I took the sack which was in my fellow-servant's possession, and found the name Henry Dunkin branded on it—this is the sack, it is full of oats.
DAVID DAVIES . I was standing there—I heard the last witness call out "Police," and saw him seize the prisoner—I saw the sack on the prisoner's back—he dropped it, and I took it inside our gates.
MICHAEL MAHONEY ( police-constable M 143.) I received charge of the prisoner—as I was taking him along he asked me how I thought he would get on—I said I did not know, I knew nothing of the case—he said, "He did not see us take it, but he saw me with it"—I asked who was with him—he said, "Young Cook."
GEORGE TERRY . I am in the service of Henry Dunkin. He had a barge lying at that wharf—I took into it eighty sacks of oats on Tuesday evening,
the 20th of April—I left it about eight o'clock—I returned at nine, and missed one sack—this is it—the oats in it correspond, as nearly as possible, with this sample taken from the barge.
Prisoner's Defence. A man asked me to carry the sack, and put it on his cart.
DANIEL DAVIES re-examined. He did not tell me that a man had employed him—he ran away.
GUILTY . Aged 23.— Confined Six Months.

1311. GEORGE WHITE and GEORGE WILLIAMS were indicted for stealing 68lbs. weight of lead, value 8 s. 6 d.; the good of Edward Herring, their master.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
JOSHUA GOLDER ( police-constable M 87.) On the evening of the 27th of April I received information, and watched the premises of Mr. Herring, in Trinity-street, Southwark—about half-past seven o'clock I saw the workman leaving and saw the prisoners coming out with the others—they went down Trinity-street—I followed them down several streets to the Waterloo-road—I noticed White had something bulky about his person—he had a jacket on, and walked heavily—I called Brown—I stopped White, and saw something fall from Williams—I took White to the station, and found this lead on his person inside his trowsers, suspended to a belt round his loins, and there were two nails to fix it to the belt—I found on him a knife, some money, and a key.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you remain in the same street with White? A. Yes; William was turning the corner—I could see where he went to—he had not time to turn the corner—he passed the "perpendicular" part of the corner—I saw him drop the lead, and a boy picked it up.
WILLIAM BROWN ( police sergeant L 9.) I was on the spot, and Golder called my attention—I saw the two prisoners—I took hold of William at the corner of Baron's-building—I said, "You have some property about you that does not belong to you"—he unbuttoned his flap, and this piece of lead fell from him—I called a boy who took it up, it weight 38lbs.—the piece found on White weight 34lbs.—Williams had a belt.
HENRY DAVISON . I live in May's-building, St. Martin's-lane, and am a plumber—the prisoner were in my employ at these chemical works in Southwark—this lead is Mr. Harring's—it was on the premises there, I have no doubt—the prisoner, and all the men there, had access to it.
Cross-examined. Q. How many partners has Mr. Harring? A. I do not know—I believe none—I never said it belonged to him and his partners—this is my signature to this deposition.
Q. Here it says, "It belonged to Mr. Herring and his partners?" A. I was not aware that I swore he had partners—it was not my lead—I have seen lead on the premises—I will not swear that I have seen this lead there—I have seen lead there of the same quality, but not rolled up like this.
EDWARD HERRING . I do not know whether the prisoners were in my service I have no partner.
Cross-examined. Q. How came it to be reporter that you had partners A. I do not know—it is called "The British Alkali Company"—I believe this lead to be mine—I am the proprietor of the establishment.
WHITE — GUILTY . Aged 33.
WILLIAMS — GUILTY . Aged 23.
Confined Twelve Months.

1318. WALTER GRIGG and GEORGE AUSTIN were indicted for stealing two loaves of bread, value 10 d.; the good of Richard Trew, from the person of Charlotte Trew.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the property of Charlotte Trew.
GRIGG pleaded GUILTY . Aged 17.— confined one month.
CHARLOTTE TREW . I am the daughter of Richard Trew. JAMES ROOTS . I am groom to Mr. Wilson. I came up, and Charlotte Trew was crying—Austin was standing there—Grigg ran down a turning—I ran to take him, but he got off—he had no bread then—the policeman came up—showed him which way the boy ran, and he saw the prisoners and another sitting in valley—we went and brought Austin—Grigg ran away—the policeman brought him off the farm, and he showed us where they had put the bread.
EMANUEL STOCKLEY ( police-constable V 291.) I found the prisoners—Austin said, he never touched the girl, and had nothing to do with it.
AUSTIN — NOT GUILTY .
