1319. JAMES SMITH and GEORGE ANDERSON were indicted for stealing 1 iron shaft, value 3 l.; and 1 iron wheel, 1 l. the goods of Charles kneene and others—2nd COUNT, stating it to be 378lbs. weight of iron.
MR. METACALF conducted the Prosecution.
BENJAMINE NICHOLLS . I am in partnership with Mr. Charles Keene and Christopher Nicholls—we have a manufactory in Granby-place, York-road, Lambeth—the way to it is through a pair of gates, through a passage, and then through another pair of gates to a yard, formerly in possession of Mr. Baker, which has been lately bought by the south-Western Railway Company this piece of iron was a shaft—it is our property—I ordered it to be placed in that yard—it was taken out of the store, and left in the vacant ground about the 8th or 10th of April—I think I last saw it safe on Thursday afternoon, the 15th of April, and on the Monday following I missed that and a packing-case—in consequence of what I heard, I endeavoured to discover where the packing-case and shaft were gone—I went to the premises with an officer, from there to a field, and from thence to the back of a house, some distance off, where I found the packing-case—I saw the prisoner Anderson there—I asked him where the shaft was—he declared he did not know, neither had he seen it—I told him what shaft I meant, and asked him four or five times, and he constantly denied it—the officer, John Ould, was with me—I went the next day to Mr. Knowlden's, in Guildford-street, and the shaft was shown to me—I am quit sure it was mine—Anderson was afterwards brought to me in custody by Ould—Anderson said it was a bad job, he hoped I would let him go for that night, it was half-past nine o'clock, and he
wished I would not give him into custody, as he said got important papers and keys which he wished to give up to Mr. Dalrymple, his employer—he said he would be there by nine o'clock in the morning—the shaft is worth 3 l. or 4 i.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you know a person of the name of Ashlin? A. Yes; I do not know that he was employed on those premises—I have heard so—Anderson admitted that the packing-case had✗ been removed by him, and gave a reason for removing it; but with reference✗ to the shaft, he said he had nothing to do with removing it.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Ashlin is an engineer? A. I do not know—he is foreman of the Lambeth Water-works Company—the South-Western Railway Company got possession of these premised three or four weeks before that time—there had been nothing sold by auction on the premises, but a great many things had been removed—there was no confsi✗ at all—this shaft was partly on the South-Western Railway premises and partly on our—no one could get it without the key to go us✗ that yard—we had one key—a man who had a stable there had a key, but he gave his key up, and a stone-mason, that had been in the yard three or four years, had one—I do not know Oakes, I have seen him, and he said he had command to remove those things that were on the premises of the✗ South-Western Railway.
MR. METCALF. Q. Were these things sufficiently on your premises to✗ indicate that they belonged to your warehouse? A. Yes; quite so—the shaft was leaning against the wall of our premises—anybody would has✗ assumed that it belonged to our warehouse—I went to the garden and was✗ Oakes there—Anderson afterwards admitted that he gave orders to Oakes to✗ remove it.
JOSEPH ASHLIN . I am foreman to the Lambeth Water-works Company. I have know the prisoner some time—I have known Anderson but a short time✗—On Friday, the 16th of April, I was working in Granby-place, taking up some water pipes, and Anderson came to me with some keys in his hand—he asked me if I would step a little way him—he took me to a gate which had a padlock on—he said on the way, that he wanted to point out to me where the thieves had been breaking in—he pointed to this piece of iron that as✗ there, and said, "They will have that before morning," and he asked if I knew in what way he could get it removed—I said, "I do not know, indeed"—he said, "Are you going to remain here all day"—I said, "Yes,"—he said, "Perhaps you will see some one who will move it"—in going back we met✗ Smith, and asked him if he would remove it—he said, "Yes," and Anderson gave him instructions to remove it—it was a round piece of iron—I did not take notice of it—we went together and Smith said, "Is this it?" Anderson said, "Yes"—Smith then said, "We had better have a drop of something to drink"—I did not see anything more of the iron—I afterwards saw Jones✗ and Parsons came running to their work—I asked them, where they had been—they said, to help a man with a piece of iron.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. While you were drinking, you men were running away with the piece of iron? A. It was some time after that I saw the me—I would not have sanctioned it—my men told me they gaw✗ it a lift—I was not at that time drinking in the public-house, I was with any✗ men—I was not in the public-house a minute—I do not know how Smith could say in truth that I gave him the iron to sell—what I have stated is the truth—I heard Smith say in the Police-court that I was the person who led
him to where the iron was—I was rather uneasy about this matter—I was 150 yards off—I was not there when the iron went away—after the statement were all given in I had a chat with Ould—I did not ask him to call at my house—I might have said, "Perhaps you will give me a call before you go to the Court"—I might have said that word to him—I will not say whether I did or not—I wanted to see him for a particular reason—because he made an omission in his statement before the Magistrate—I did not ask him to tell that on the second examination—he might do as he thought proper—he did not call at my house, to my knowledge—I never saw him there—he has been to my house with Anderson—he did not call after my observation to him—when I said he might give me a call, it was one evening in the York-road, by the Rising Sun—I did not hear that he had called after that—I do not know that he was treated at my house—I am not knowledgeable of his having anything to drink at my house—I have never heard it.
Q. What was it the policeman omitted to say? A. He omitted saying that Anderson said, "Ashlin, I am done for, for Gods sake intercede with the policeman to let me go until the morning"—I said, "You have been an officer, you know better than that; if this man gives you up, he is liable to lose his situation"—I did not want anything set right—I had no object at all—I am nor the prosecutor of this indictment—I am not supplying any of the funds—I do not know who is—I never had a key to this yard.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Anderson asked you if you could get him some one to remove this iron? A. Yes, that occurred in the yard where the shaft was—he had previously pointed out the shaft—I did not know exactly on whose premises it was—it might belong to the Railway Company—my two men went to help in putting it on the truck—Anderson and I had a drop of gin together—I do not know what Smith had, it was something in a pot—my men are named Jones and Parsons—this happened about one o'clock in the day—Oake is porter to one of the auctioneers who had been selling the property—I was not aware whether Oakes had been helping the iron into the truck—I was not there—the public-house is about fifty yards from the yard—I think the gates were left open—there was not any one in the yard when we were in.
JAMES MILBURY . I am in Mr. Nicholls' employ. I was in the stable in Mr. Baker's yard, which adjoins Mr. Nicholls' premises, on that day—I saw a shaft of iron in the yard—I saw five men go and take it out—they were Smith, Parsons, Jones, Oakes, and a boy—they brought it out, put it on the truck, and took it right away—Smith assisted in dragging the truck.
JOHN KNOWLDEN . I live with my father, who is an engineer, in Great Guildford-street. On the 16th of April Smith came to me with some old iron, a piece of a shaft, and a rigger—he offered it for sale for 8 s. a cwt.—I offered him 7 s. 6 d., which he agreed to take—he took some old iron, and paid me the difference—I showed that shaft afterwards to Mr. Nicholls.
JOHN OULD ( police-constable L 87.) I was called by Mr. Nicjolls—I went to a garden, and found a packing-case—Anderson was there—he deniced seeing the shaft—I saw the shaft at Knowlden's—I took Smith into custody—he made no reply at first, but he afterwards asked me who gave me information—I said I found it out by making inquiries—Milbury afterwards came, and identified him as the man he had seen with the truck and the shait✗—next day, on the way to the station, Smith said he bought it of a man whom he knew two years ago, but he could give nodesription of his name or address—on the 25th of April, I took Andreson—I said I wanted him respecting an iron shaft—he said, "Ashlin is as deep in the mud, as I am in the mire"—I forgot myself at the examination before the Magistrate, but when we got to Ashlin's, Anderson said, "What a pretty mess you have brought me into, you have done this to screen yourself; you are as deep in the mad✗ as I am in the
mire"—he said he thought it hard to be locked up, and not Ashlin—Ashlin said, "You know you have been a constable yourself, and if the constable gives you up, he may leave his situation"—after we left Mr. Ashlin, Anderson said he would split about it to-morrow—I said, all we wanted was the truth—he asked me to go to Mr. Nicholls'—we called there, and he begged and prayed of Mr. Nicholls to let him go—he said he was a constable, he had got keys and papers to deliver up, and he would appear any time to-morrow that was necessary.
Cross-examined. by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You did not happen to remember some things on the first examination? A. I have not so good a headpiece as some people—I mentioned about his "slitting" the second time, not the first—I do not know whether Ashlin was uneasy about my forgetting it—he said I had mistaken it—I called at his house, but I did not see him—he was not at home—I saw a person, I do not know whether it was his wife or not—she acted like a domestic woman—I believe I had some ale—I did not pay for it—that was the day before I went to the Police-court, and then the next day I told about the "splitting," and about the mud and the mire.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. Smith is a dealer in old iron? A. He is a collector of it—he said at the station, after the charge was written down, "What, am I charged with stealing it?" MR. METCALF. Q. How much ale had you? A. One pint.
HENRY BATEMAN HODSON . I am assistant surveyor to the London and South-Western Railway Company. they are extending their line from Nine✗ Elms—they have lately bought this yard, of Mr. Baker—Anderson was in the employ of the Company—he had authority to remove iron, or lead, or any stores belonging to the Company, to a place of safety, or into empty houses, where he could lock them up, and where they would be secure.
MR. BALLANTINE to JOSEPH ASHLIN. Q. Did you go to an attorney's office for Smith, and instruct him to appear on his behalf? A. No; I went to a solicitor on my own behalf.
COURT. Q. What time did Anderson come to you? A. At past twelve o'clock in the day.
COURT to JAMES MILLBURY. Q. What time did Smith come with the others to take these things? A. I think it was after dinner, a little after two o'clock—I did not see Anderson.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470510-1320]
1320. JOHN ROBINSON was indicted for stealing 50 lbs. weight of iron, value 7 s., the goods of James Little and others; and JAMES GUTTERIDGE for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing it to have been stolen.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS WEST ( police-constable M 249.) On the evening of the 23rd April, about a quarter before nine o'clock, I was in Wellington-street, Dock-head,—I saw Robinson coming in a direction from the Greenwich railway with a quantity of hoop iron on his shoulder—I followed him to the prisoner Gutteridge's ware-house, in Jacob-street, Dock-head—he threw the iron down at Gutteridge's warehouse door—he then went to Gutteridge's private house, about three doors from the warehouse—he went inside, and staid about two minutes—Guttridge then let him out—he fetched the iron from the warehouse door, and Gutteridge let him into the private house with it—he remained there about five minutes—I then saw him come out without the bundle—he went on, and
I passed him under a gas-lamp—he was in the act of counting money—he went on, and I followed him to the King's Arms public-house—Whitlamb there spoke to him, and asked if he had taken any iron to Gutteridge's—he said he had, and he fetched it from the foundry, by the King and Queen, Rother-hithe—(that was a different direction to that in which I had seen him come)—I took him into custody, and handed him to a constable on the beat—we went to Gutteridge, and asked him if he had sent a man to the foundry—he said, "No"—I asked if he had bought any iron—he said he had, about a week ago, of a man with a smock frock—I asked if he had bought any iron since—he said he had not—I asked if he had received any that day or night—he said he had not—I said I should search the place—I went through to the back door, and opened it—I saw the bundle lying at the door—I said, "Mr. Gutteridge, here is some iron here"—he said, "That is the iron I bought a week ago"—I searched, and found no other iron—I believe that was the iron that Robinson had had—I then took Gutteridge to the station—I saw Robinson there—I asked Gutteridge if that was the man he had seen a week ago—he said he believed it was—before we took him he said he had bought the iron of a man, and gave him 1 s. 6 d. and a pint of beer, and the man asked him more than old iron price for it—that he bought it of a short man with a smock-frock on.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had Robinson a smock-frock on? A. Yes.
JOHN WHITLAMB ( police-constable M 89.) I was with West, on the evening of the 23rd of April—I saw Robinson coming from Dock-head with this bundle of iron hoops—I followed him, till he laid them down at the door of Mr. Gutteridge's cooperage—I then saw him go to the door of Mr. Gutteridge's residence—the door was opened—he went in, and came out, picked up the iron, and I followed him to the door and saw Mr. Gutteridge standing with a light—I afterwards went there with West, and found a bundle of iron in the back yard—to the best of my belief it was the same I had seen before—I was at the station when the prisoners were there together—I heard Gutteridge say, he believed Robinson was the man that wore the smock-frock that he had the iron of a week ago—Robinson had a smock-frock on.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe the Magistrate said it was a case fit to be inquired into, and held Gutteridge to bail? A. Yes.
JEREMIAH LYON . I am a watchman, belonging to the South Eastern Railway Company, near the Greenwich line—I am servant to James Little and Sons—I have seen this bundle of iron—they had such property on their premises—one of the sons died lately, some time before the 23rd of April—they had a quantity of this hoop iron for some months before the 23rd of April—I have seen it frequently—I had seen it in the early part of that night—I did not miss any till I was asked by the policeman if I missed any—I know Robinson.
JOSIAH WILLIAM BROWN . I am foreman to Messrs. James Little and Sons—one of the sons died lately, but there are now two sons and the father—they are partners—one of the sons is the principal person in the business, in fact he may be called the only contractor for this property—this belongs to them.
Cross-examined. Q. Has not one of the sons died lately? A. Yes, about two months since, but there are two sons now living—they are partners with the father—the business is always known in the name of James Little and Sons.
COURT. Q. What is the value of this iron? A. Seven shillings—it is not old iron—it weighs half-a-cwt.—I should say that a cooper who is in the habit of using iron would know old iron from new—this has never been untied.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Suppose it were old iron, what would be the value of it? A. I do not know—I only know the value of new—this had been on our premises I suppose for two months—it came from Hill and Perry's, is Whitechapel.
(The prisoner Gutteridge received a good character.)
ROBINSON GUILTY .—Aged 49.— Confined Three Months.
GUTTERIDGE — GUILTY. Recommended to mercy by the Jury. Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18470510-1321]
1321. JOHN WILLIAMS and HENRY DAWSON were indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 25 s., and 1 pair of trowsers, 10 s.; the goods of James Digby; and that they had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES DIGBY . I keep a shop in High-street, Wandsworth—I had a coat and a pair of trowsers safe at seven o'clock in the evening on the 20th of April, and I missed them from my front shop afterwards—these are them.
JOHN MONKTON ( police-constable V 197.) I found this coat and trowser on the prisoner Dawson, on the 20th of April—Williams was in company with him when they were given into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Which of the prisoners did you see first? A. Williams—Cox had hold of him—Dawson was about two yards off—he was brought back by two other persons, who said, "This is the other man"—there were twenty people assembled—I had heard a cry of "Police," and went up.
THOMAS COX . I am a plasterer—I live in High-street, Wandsworth. Ah half-past eight o'clock in the evening, on the 20th of April, I was sitting at my window, and I saw the two prisoners come up the town—they stood about the prosecutor's shop—Williams went in, and Dawson stood a little way below—a constable came by, and Dawson went up and gave Williams a nudge on the elbow—the officer passed by, and then Williams went into the shop again, and came out with something on his arm, and Dawson joined him.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Where were you? A. Sitting up at my window, on the opposite side of the street, about twelve yards off—I looked down on the prisoners—I had not seen either of them before—I followed them—I had not lost sight of them above five or then minutes.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Did you see the coat on the prisoner Dawson? A. Yes, I saw it take off his back at the station—he had three coats on.
JAMES DRISCOLL ( police-sergeant C 2.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner Dawson's former conviction at Clerkenwell, by the name of James Thomas—( read—Convicted 6th of Oct., 1845, and confined one months)—he is the person.
JOSEPH HUGHES ( police-constable E 114.) I produce a certificate of Williams's conviction at Clerkenwell, by the name of William Steven—( read—Convicted 25th of March, 1846, and confined six months)—Williams is the person—he has been twice summarily convicted since.
WILLIAMS — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
DAWSON — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18470510-1321a]
1321a. ARTHUR HARGIN was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 2 l.; 1 watch-guard chain, 2 s.; 1 half-sovereign, and 2 sixpences; the goods of Thomas Parker, from his person.
MR. HORRY conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS PARKER . I came from Hull, in Yorkshire—I was living at Peter's-hill, Doctors' Commons. On the 17th of April, I was going over London-bridge, I was spoken to by a person, and in consequence of what he said I went to a house in King-street, Southwark, No. 4, I think—I went up stairs with him—I saw the prisoner in the room up stairs—the prisoner introduced this piece of satin—( looking at it)—he asked me to purchase it—he said it was valued at 30 s.—I told him I had not the money to purchase anything of the kind—he came down to 6 s., and I purchased it, with the idea of clearing out of the house—while I was paying for it, this shawl ( looking at it) was introduced—the prisoner opened a chest in the room, and took it out—he said it was valued at 17 l.—I told him I had not 17 s. by me, I could not Purchase anything of the kind—he asked me if I would show him my watch—I pulled it out, and showed it him—after he saw that, I told him how much money I had—he would not believe me, so I pulled my money out, and showed it him—I had one half-sovereign and three sixpences—he took the half-sovereign out of my hand, and two of the sixpences, and left me one sixpence—he had put the shawl into my hat, and I had thrown it out two or three times, and put it on the table—he then unbuttoned a button of my waistcoat, and took my watch out, and while I was looking at it he slipped the guard over my neck.
COURT. Q. Had you given him authority to take that watch, either directly or indirectly? A. No; it was only through fear—the stair-foot door opens by a spring—the prisoner told me if the door was fast when I got below, I was to give three knocks, so (and he gave three knocks, rather loud)—I ran down stairs as fast as I could, and the door was wide open—I gave information, and saw the prisoner two days afterwards—I did not part with my watch willingly—I have the key of it in my pocket, and I should have given the key if I had parted with it willingly.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. what age are you? A. I am twenty-three years old—I was a truck man or car man in Hull—there was some intimationthat these things were smuggled—this is the silk and the shawl—this all passed in the presence of the other man; and he kept talking to me, and told me I must make a good bargain with this man, as he was drunk—he made a number of different remarks—my watch was worth about 2 l.
HENRY BARRY ( police-constable M 171.) I met the prosecutor in the street, on the 17th April—he told me he had been robbed of his watch—I looked after the prisoner—I found him on the 19th of April, and took him into custody—he was pointed out to me by the prosecutor—I told the prisoner he must go to the station with me, for robbing this man✗ of his watch—he said, "Very well, I will go with you"—he went a little way, and he took some-thing out of his pocket and attempted to hand it to another man—I did not choose that—I took his hand, and we had a fight—I got him to the station, and found a number of duplicates in his hand.
Prisoner. If I had been guilty of anything of the kind. the prosecutor would have given me in charge in the open street.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18470510-1322]
1322. JAMES DORE was indicted for embezzlement.
JOHN SIMPSON . I am a ginger-beer and soda-water maker—I live in King's-place, Newington—the prisoner was in my service—it was his duty to take money and bring it to me when he came home at night—if he received the sums in question he ought to have paid them to me on the days he received them.
Prisoner. When you cast up my wages on Saturday night you said, "You have had so much money of me, "and deducted it. Witness. No, I did not.
Prisoner. I never received this money—I always gave receipts.
EDWARD FRANCIS . I paid the prisoner 1 s. for Mr. Simpson, on the 3rd of April—I have no receipt for it—I paid ready money.
HENRY WILLIAM BARTON . I had a dozen of ginger-beer, and three bottles of soda-water of the prisoner—I paid him 1 s. 10 1/2 d. for it—I did not pay him at the time I had it, but I am quite sure it was paid—I am not indebted to Mr. Simpson for anything.
Prisoner. You were not at home; your wife took them in, and said, "When my husband is in the way, he will pay you. Witness. I am well aware of that, but I paid you the next day when you called.
COURT. Q. Are you sure you paid him? A. I would not swear it, but I believe I did.
HENRY BUTTLE . I had six bottles of lemonade, and six bottles of soda-water from the prisoner—my wife paid him in my presence—I gave her the money—I cannot say on what day it was—there was a receipt given for it, but it is lost.
Prisoner's Defence. I have been about the town for Mr. Simpson for fourteen months; another person set up in the business; he heard of me, and offered me 5 s. a week more than I had of Mr. Simpson; I said I was satisfied, I did not want to change; this is done out of spite, because the other gentleman wished to get me away.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470510-1323]
1323. JAMES DORE was again indicted for stealing 12 bottles, value 1 s., and 3 quarts of ginger-beer, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Simpson, his master.
JOHN SIMPSON . On the 3rd of April I received information that the prisoner was taking out more goods than he accounted for, and on the 4th of April I watched him, and saw that he was doing so—I told the inspector—he told me to watch him on the following morning, and I did—I saw the prisoner and the foreman put the goods into the cart—the prisoner took the cart into the street—it was then taken to the station, and I found in it one dozen more than the prisoner had accounted for—he had done it by a very novel mode of packing—in the bottom tier of the crate he had managed to put four dozen and three bottles, four dozen and three in the middle tier, and in the top tier only four dozen—the officer found that also, and in consequence of that I gave him into custody.
Prisoner. When the crates were empty we went to the stock of beer and filled them; when they were full we thought there were twelve dozen; we never counted them.
CHARLES MANN . I drive for Mr. Simpson. I took out the boxes with the Prisoner—they ought to have held four dozen in each tier—he ordered me to unpack one box that we did not sell, and there were six bottles over—I told him it was wrong, and I would not allow it while he was out with me—he said it was all right, it was only six over.
Prisoner. Q. Did they go back to the factory? A. Yes, but then he took the money—we took out three boxes, and there were six over in each.
JOHN WRIGHT ( police-constable M 63.) I saw the prisoner, on the 5th of April—I took charge of the two boxes of bottles—I found fifty-one bottles in each of the two bottom tiers, and forty-eight in the other—there were six more bottles than there ought to be.
JOHN SIMPSON re-examined. He brought these bottles back as if he had not sold them, when he had sold others instead of them.
Prisoner. I borrowed to loans of 20 l. for him—I borrowed them for my master—I am answerable for it—here is the letter sent to me about it—he said he would do everything to serve me. Witness. I am answerable for it, not him—I am to pay it back by instalments.
COURT. Q. How came the man to borrow money for you? A. I was short of money—it requires one person to borrow it, and another to be security.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18470510-1324]
1324. WILLIAM WESTON was indicated for stealing two same rabbits, value 4 s.; the goods of JANE WEBB; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
JANE WEBB . I am the wife of John Webb—we live at Lambeth—we had two tame rabbits safe on the 7th of May—we missed them—this is one of them—I can swear to it—they were safe on Friday night, and gone on the Saturday.
RICHARD HOLWAY . I live in East-street, Lambeth. About five o'clock on Saturday afternoon, the 8th of May, the prisoner came to me, and asked if I would buy two rabbits—I said I could sell them for him—I took them to Ingram, and he bought them.
WILLIAM INGRAM . I bought these rabbits of Holway.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought them on the Saturday, between three and four o'clock; I gave 1 s. 6 d. for them; I asked Holway to buy them; he said he had left off keeping them, but he could sell them for me.
HENRY BUCKLEY ( police-constable G 73.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction at this Court—( read—Convicted 30th March, 1846, having been before convicted of felony, confined one year)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Transported for seven Years.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JUNE 14 TH, 1847.
